That closeness makes the question more direct. Hūd does not address an unknown population through abstract theory. He speaks to people who know his character and can recognize the language of their own community in his words. Their rejection cannot be explained by saying that the messenger did not understand them.

The word brotherly does not soften the truth into approval. Hūd asks whether they will fear Allah because he wants them rescued from a direction that will harm them. The question is severe because the danger is severe, but its source is concern rather than hatred.

A messenger's belonging also gives his warning a human voice. The people can hear both judgment of their actions and hope for their return. Hūd is not celebrating their weakness or waiting for their collapse. He is asking them to recognize God before their power becomes a cause of ruin.

**A Verified Parallel Gives Hūd's Exact Setting**

Quran 7:65 records the same address to ʿĀd: Hūd calls them to worship Allah alone and asks whether they will not fear Him. The parallel confirms that the present question is the core of his mission, not a passing comment within a larger political dispute.

Quran 46:21 places his warning among the sand-hills and notes that warners had come before and after him. Hūd's brotherhood therefore joins local knowledge with universal guidance. He is one of them, yet he speaks in continuity with the messengers.

People sometimes assume that a message from a stranger is easier to reject because the stranger lacks credibility. Hūd's people show the opposite difficulty. They know him, yet they still refuse the question. Familiarity can become a reason to dismiss a person as ordinary rather than to consider the truth he brings.

The messenger's closeness removes one excuse but does not remove the heart's freedom to resist. A community can know a person's honesty and still dislike the command he conveys. Hūd's task is to remain faithful even when the people use their shared history as a reason not to listen.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:125

> I am truly a trustworthy messenger to you

**Hūd Grounds His Call in Trustworthiness**

Hūd describes himself as a trustworthy messenger. The statement identifies both the quality of the bearer and the authority of the mission. He is not presenting a personal theory about ʿĀd or seeking influence through a new religious program. He has been entrusted with a message from God and is committed to conveying it faithfully.

Trustworthiness matters because the call requires the people to challenge their worship, power, and way of life. They need to know whether Hūd is manipulating them for money, status, or private control. His declaration answers the suspicion before it becomes an excuse. He speaks as a servant under God, not as an ambitious rival to the chiefs.

Hūd's brotherhood means the people have had the opportunity to observe him. He is not an unknown voice whose character they cannot assess. His life among them gives weight to the claim of trustworthiness. The community can compare his public conduct with the message he now brings.

Familiarity does not automatically prove a person's truth, but it removes the argument that Hūd appeared without a history. A messenger's known honesty creates a fair basis for hearing him. If the people reject him, they must address the message rather than invent a motive that his life does not support.

**The Message Requires Moral Courage From the Bearer**

A trustworthy messenger cannot edit the warning to protect his reputation. Hūd must tell ʿĀd that their power, construction, and violence are subject to God's judgment. Such speech risks the anger of chiefs who benefit from the existing order. His trustworthiness includes the courage to say what the people need rather than what will preserve his comfort.

Courage does not mean enjoying confrontation. Hūd speaks because the people need guidance, not because he wants to defeat them. The messenger's firmness serves mercy. If he hid the danger, his politeness would become betrayal of the trust placed in him.

Quran 11:51 records Hūd telling ʿĀd that he asks no reward for the message and that his reward is with the One who created him. That parallel adds a concrete sign of his trustworthiness. He is not seeking money from the people whose beliefs he challenges.

Freedom from payment does not mean the message has no value. It means its value is not sold to the audience. Hūd's reward is with God, so the chiefs cannot buy his silence and the poor cannot be excluded for lacking payment. The mission remains open to the whole people.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:126

> So fear Allah, and obey me

**The Command Follows the Messenger's Claim**

Hūd has identified himself as a trustworthy messenger. This verse now states the response that trustworthiness requires: reverence God and obey the messenger. The command is not an invitation to admire Hūd's character while leaving life unchanged. His reliability matters because his guidance must be followed.

The order is also significant. Reverence for Allah comes before obedience to Hūd. The messenger is not the final object of loyalty; he directs the people to their Lord. They obey Hūd because he conveys God's command, and their obedience to him is therefore an expression of obedience to God rather than a transfer of worship to a human being.

To fear Allah is not to imagine Him as an unpredictable force waiting to harm the people. Reverence combines awareness of His greatness with caution about disobedience. It makes the heart conscious that power, wealth, and public honor remain under His judgment. The fear Hūd calls for is therefore a protection from arrogance.

A community that has become strong may fear only visible enemies. It measures danger by armies, droughts, rivals, or economic loss. Hūd redirects its attention to the Lord who gave it every capacity. When that awareness is restored, the people can use their strength without treating it as permission to do whatever they wish.

**A Repeated Prophetic Pattern**

The same pairing appears in the surrounding narratives. Noah tells his people to fear God and obey him, and Hūd now receives the identical duty. The repetition shows continuity in the prophetic mission. Hūd is not asking ʿĀd to accept a novel religious demand; he is renewing the summons that earlier peoples heard.

Quran 7:65 records Hūd calling ʿĀd to worship Allah and asking whether they will not fear Him. The parallel confirms that the present command joins worship, reverence, and obedience. The three belong together: worship identifies the true Lord, reverence guards the heart, and obedience proves the claim in life.

For a weak person, obedience may seem like a simple private choice. For a dominant society, it has public consequences. If ʿĀd obeys Hūd, its chiefs can no longer treat their power as unrestricted. They must answer for how they build, attack, judge, and distribute what they have.

That is why a powerful people may resist an apparently short command. The words reach into the foundations of its identity. Reverence for God places limits on rulers, traders, builders, and warriors. It tells every one of them that success is not a license and that another authority stands above the social order.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:127

> I do not ask you for any reward for this ˹message˺. My reward is only from the Lord of all worlds

**The Messenger Removes a Suspicion**

Hūd has asked a powerful people to change its worship and conduct. Such a demand can provoke the accusation that the messenger is pursuing a private benefit. This verse answers that suspicion directly. He does not seek payment from ʿĀd for conveying the warning. His service is not a commercial arrangement in which acceptance purchases his approval.

The statement does not make the message less valuable. It makes its value independent of the audience's ability or willingness to pay. A poor person can hear the same warning as a chief, and a chief cannot buy a softer version. The message comes with equal force to every member of the people.

**A Verified Parallel Establishes the Same Principle**

Quran 11:51 records Hūd telling ʿĀd that he asks no reward and that his reward is with the One who created him. The parallel confirms that the absence of payment is part of his public claim, not a detail invented by later readers. His mission is grounded in dependence on the Creator rather than on the community.

The Qur'anic pattern also appears in the call of Noah and in the other prophetic accounts of this sūrah. The repeated refusal of payment distinguishes revelation from a scheme for social control. A messenger may be opposed, but he does not place salvation behind a fee or sell access to God.

**The Lord of All Worlds Is the Proper Recipient of Hope**

By naming God as the Lord of all worlds, Hūd places his reward with the One who owns every audience and every outcome. The people of ʿĀd may control one region, but they do not control the creation. Their approval is local and temporary. God's knowledge and recompense encompass the messenger, the people, and the consequences of their choices.

This title also prevents tribal bargaining. Hūd is not saying, “I ask no reward from you because another tribe will pay me.” His hope is in the Lord of every people. The call is therefore universal in its moral scope, even while he speaks to one particular society.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:128

> ˹Why˺ do you build a landmark on every high place in vanity

**A Question About Visible Construction**

Hūd turns from the inner question of worship to the public face of ʿĀd's society. He asks why they place a landmark on every high place in vanity. Their construction is not presented as a neutral achievement. It has become a statement intended to be seen, admired, and associated with the power of its builders.

The question invites the people to examine purpose rather than merely technique. A building can shelter, connect, or benefit a community. It can also become a display that demands attention from everyone who passes. Hūd challenges the motive behind the monument, not the human ability to shape a landscape.

A high place naturally gives a structure visibility. The landmark can be seen from far away, so it announces the reach of the people who raised it. In a society already confident in its strength, repeated public signs may feed the belief that greatness consists in being noticed and feared.

The Qur'anic wording permits a linguistic range: the Arabic term for the high place can also be associated with a route or path. The exact form of every structure is not necessary to the warning. Whether the object was a monument beside a road or another prominent construction, the moral issue is the vanity of making achievement a public badge of superiority.

**A Parallel Recalls ʿĀd's Stature**

Quran 89:6–8 recalls how God dealt with ʿĀd and associates them with Iram and great stature. That passage confirms the broader memory of a people known for impressive presence. It does not require later readers to identify every ancient ruin or assign a modern site to each landmark. The revealed point is the contrast between their imposing reputation and their eventual accountability.

The high-place image therefore works as moral geography. Their pride rises before the eyes, but no height raises it beyond God's judgment. A visible achievement may dominate a horizon for a generation while remaining insignificant before the Lord who created the horizon.

A community may rightly build roads, bridges, homes, places of worship, and defenses. The verse asks what public works say about the people who control them. Do they increase safety, access, and justice, or do they make the poor pay for the prestige of rulers? Do they preserve the land, or exhaust it for a monument?

Hūd's challenge is therefore relevant to every organized society. Construction is moral when it answers real needs and acknowledges limits. A landmark becomes vanity when its primary function is to make others submit to the builders' image of themselves.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:129

> and construct castles, as if you are going to live forever

**A Later Passage Describes the End of Their Security**

Quran 46:24–25 describes the punishment approaching ʿĀd's valleys and says that the destructive wind left only their dwellings visible. This parallel gives a sober answer to the imagined permanence in the present verse. Structures may remain as traces, but the people who assumed their own endurance do not remain within them.

The account does not invite speculation about identifying every ruin. Its force is moral and certain: what appears secure can be stripped of its inhabitants by God's command. A surviving wall is not proof that its builders escaped judgment. It can instead be a witness to the limits of human security.

A believer is not asked to be careless about tomorrow. The question is what kind of future planning deserves the greatest attention. ʿĀd plans for continued residence in this world while ignoring the accountability that follows it. Their architecture is extensive, but their moral horizon is narrow.

To plan well is to provide for people without forgetting the Provider. It is to build institutions that can be judged by justice, not merely by endurance. It is to ask whether a project will remain beneficial when its founders are gone and whether its costs have been imposed fairly on those with less power.

A fortified environment may insulate a people from the suffering it causes. Those inside see comfort and strength; those outside experience exclusion. When the walls prevent self-examination, the castle becomes more than a building. It becomes a system that keeps correction away from the powerful.

Hūd's warning breaks through that insulation. The people must recognize that no wall can conceal their conduct from God. The Lord sees the purpose of the construction, the labor used to raise it, and the harms committed in its name. A castle can hide a person from neighbors but never from divine knowledge.

A structure's age does not prove the righteousness of its builders. A famous monument may survive while its society's injustice is forgotten by its admirers. God's judgment evaluates what human admiration cannot see: intention, oppression, gratitude, and obedience.

This distinction frees the servant from envy of impressive power. Another person's palace may look permanent, but appearance is not security. The true question is whether the builder has prepared to meet the Lord. A modest shelter raised with gratitude can be more valuable than a vast fortress raised in denial.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:130

> and act so viciously when you attack ˹others˺

**Strength Becomes Corrupt When It Has No Accountability**

The earlier verses show a people with resources, construction, and provision. None of those gifts automatically produces cruelty. The problem arises when the people interpret prosperity as permission. If they believe that their success proves their superiority, they may treat every weaker group as an object to be controlled.

Fear of Allah would interrupt that logic. The powerful person remembers that the same Lord who grants strength can judge its use. Every blow, order, seizure, and threat is known. Accountability turns power from a private possession into a trust whose exercise must be justified.

Quran 41:15 records ʿĀd boasting about their greater strength and asks who could be mightier than they. That parallel helps explain the violence challenged here. A people convinced that it is unsurpassed can mistake victory for moral right and resistance for an insult that deserves punishment.

The Qur'anic response is not to compare human groups on their own terms. It reminds them that the One who created them is mightier. The point is not that strength is shameful; it is that no created strength can authorize rebellion against the Creator or cruelty toward creation.

Violence committed by individuals is serious, but Hūd addresses a culture that normalizes it. When a society praises the conqueror, excuses the strong, and calls victims weak, aggression becomes part of its identity. The question then concerns laws, institutions, stories, and leaders, not only private tempers.

A just society must restrain force through truthful judgment. It must protect people who cannot defend themselves and must refuse to turn collective power into a license for revenge. The people of ʿĀd are challenged because their attacks show that their civilization has not produced moral maturity.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:131

> So fear Allah, and obey me

**The Call Returns to Its First Demand**

After exposing ʿĀd's vanity, imagined permanence, and violent attacks, Hūd returns to the central command: fear Allah and obey the messenger. The repetition is not a failure to advance the argument. It shows that every social problem he has named grows from the same root, a heart that no longer lives under God's authority.

The people may separate worship from architecture, politics, and warfare, as if religion concerned only private rituals. Hūd refuses that separation. Reverence must reach the way a society builds, protects itself, uses force, and treats those beyond its circle. The command returns because the whole of life must return to God.

A first summons can be dismissed as a general ideal. Repeated instruction removes that excuse. Hūd has already stated his trustworthiness and his freedom from payment. He has questioned their structures and attacks. Now the response is stated again in the same compact form so that no one can claim uncertainty about what repentance requires.

The people are not asked to decode a hidden program. They are asked to revere Allah and follow the messenger. Clarity is itself a mercy. It gives the powerful no room to pretend that they were confused about the moral direction demanded of them.

**The Messenger's Role Remains Servanthood**

Hūd repeats the command without making himself the object of the people's devotion. They are to fear Allah first. His authority comes from carrying God's message, and obedience to him is valid because it leads the people back to the Lord rather than toward Hūd's personal glorification.

This order protects the community from personality cults. A sincere messenger does not ask people to admire his identity while ignoring the Lord's claim. He asks them to hear, understand, and submit to God. Hūd's repeated phrase is therefore an act of humility as well as authority.

The same summons has appeared in Noah's story and appears in the accounts of other messengers in this sūrah. Prophetic guidance is not a collection of competing demands. Each messenger calls his people to the One God, to reverence, and to obedience. The differences in their circumstances do not change the center of the message. Quran 7:65 presents Hūd's call to ʿĀd in the same connection between worship, reverence, and obedience, confirming that this repeated command is the mission's center rather than a passing refrain.

This continuity also exposes the excuse that Hūd's call is merely an unusual opinion. His people are being asked to return to a truth that has been brought before. Repetition across prophetic histories gives the command a wider witness than the approval or rejection of any one tribe.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:132

> Fear the One Who has provided you with ˹the good˺ things you know

**Their Blessings Were Common Knowledge**

The next verses name cattle, children, gardens, and springs. The present verse prepares the people to look at those blessings as a coherent provision from one Lord. Hūd is not flattering ʿĀd with vague praise; he is identifying concrete realities they cannot reasonably deny.

The list also prevents the chiefs from claiming that only their elite experience God's favor. Animals, family, cultivation, and water support the whole community in different ways. The Provider's gifts are not proof that one class is inherently superior. They are trusts distributed through a society that must answer for how it shares them. Quran 16:5–6 likewise describes cattle through the benefits and beauty people receive from them, joining usefulness to a reminder of the Giver.

When blessings continue for a long time, they can appear normal and deserved. The recipient forgets that life could have been otherwise. Hūd's command interrupts that forgetfulness. The more familiar a benefit becomes, the more deliberately it may need to be remembered as mercy.

Entitlement is dangerous because it turns a gift into a claim against both God and people. The entitled person expects provision to continue while refusing the duties attached to it. Fear of Allah restores proportion: enjoy what is lawful, acknowledge its source, and do not use it as an excuse to disregard the Giver or harm His creation.

**Provision Does Not Confirm Every Choice**

ʿĀd could argue that its prosperity proves that God approves its way of life. Hūd's warning rejects that conclusion. A blessing can be granted as an opportunity, a test, or a temporary respite. Continued wealth is not a certificate that a society's worship and violence are right.

This point protects the reader from judging truth by immediate material success. Some people prosper while acting unjustly, and some righteous people endure hardship. The Provider gives in wisdom, while accountability concerns the response. The proper question is not only, “What do we possess?” but, “What are we doing with what we possess?”

Hūd's reasoning is relational. The command is not an arbitrary demand from a distant ruler. It is a call to respond rightly to the One whose care has sustained the people from the beginning.

If ʿĀd acknowledges God as Provider, it must abandon the fantasy of self-sufficiency. It must recognize that its buildings, attacks, and social order are answerable to the source of its life. Provision becomes a doorway to worship when the people let gratitude govern their power.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:133

> He provided you with cattle, and children

**Provision Becomes Specific**

Hūd moves from the general reminder of provision to two familiar forms: cattle and children. The examples bring theology into daily life. The people do not have to search for a hidden miracle before recognizing dependence on God. Their food, labor, wealth, family life, and future are all tied to gifts they did not create from nothing. Quran 16:5 links cattle with warmth, food, and other benefits, while 16:72 names children among God's good provisions; those parallels illuminate why Hūd chooses these ordinary signs.

The verse places animals and children together without reducing either to property. Both are blessings that involve responsibility. Cattle may support food, work, transport, or livelihood; children carry the hope of continuity and require protection, formation, and care. The gifts enlarge a people's capacity, but they also enlarge its accountability.

A society can count animals as units of prestige and economic power. Hūd asks the people to see beyond the count to the Provider. Each benefit drawn from an animal—food, clothing, labor, or means of exchange—reminds the community that its prosperity depends on life it did not manufacture.

This recognition should produce humane stewardship. If animals are treated only as instruments for increasing status, the blessing becomes another expression of the vanity already criticized. A grateful people uses what it has with restraint and care, acknowledging that dominion over creatures is limited and answerable to God.

**Children Are a Gift and a Trust**

Children are named as provision, but they are not possessions by which parents may boast. They are lives entrusted to families and communities. The gift includes the duty to feed, protect, teach, and prepare them for moral responsibility. A society that celebrates its population while neglecting the vulnerable has misunderstood the blessing.

The mention of children also exposes the emptiness of seeking permanence through descendants alone. Earlier, ʿĀd built as if it would live forever. A family line may continue after an individual dies, but it cannot make a person immortal or excuse the deeds for which he will answer. Continuity is not the same as salvation.

Cattle and children connect private life to public life. Animals may support households and markets; children depend on parents, relatives, teachers, and neighbors. God's provision therefore creates networks of obligation rather than permission for isolated accumulation.

A powerful group that hoards resources while weaker households struggle contradicts the meaning of the gift. The Provider is not honored by turning provision into a weapon. Gratitude seeks fair dealing, protection of dependents, and generosity toward people who cannot return the benefit immediately.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:134

> and gardens, and springs

**The Landscape Itself Is Named as Provision**

Hūd completes his immediate list with gardens and springs. The gifts are not confined to objects owned by a household. They include a living landscape: cultivated growth and the water that makes growth possible. ʿĀd's prosperity is therefore surrounded by signs of sustaining care.

Gardens display the fruit of time, soil, labor, and water. Springs suggest a source that continues to feed life beyond a single moment. By naming them, Hūd asks the people to look at the environment that supports their comfort and to recognize that its order is not an achievement of their pride alone. Quran 16:10–11 similarly connects rain with drinking water, grazing, crops, and fruit, presenting the chain of provision as a sign for reflection.

A garden can be enjoyed, cultivated, and shared, but it cannot be treated as an endless possession. Its fertility depends on conditions outside the gardener's total control. A grateful society protects water, soil, and access to nourishment instead of exhausting the land for immediate display.

This is not a technical rule about one agricultural method. It is a moral principle. What sustains life is a trust. The people who receive gardens should ask whether their use leaves the land capable of benefiting others and future generations. Vanity consumes resources to make the present look powerful; gratitude preserves them as gifts.

**Springs Point to Dependence**

A spring gives a visible image of provision that precedes human labor. People can channel water, build around it, and distribute it, but they do not command the source into existence. The spring reminds the people that their skill works with a gift already present.

That reminder should temper claims of ownership. Control over access to water can make rulers feel indispensable, but the source remains God's. A just community manages the means of provision for life rather than turning necessity into a tool of humiliation or domination.

When gardens remain green and springs continue to flow, a generation may assume that prosperity is automatic. The more stable the blessing appears, the easier it is to forget the One who sustains it. Hūd's list restores the origin of what the people see every day.

Forgetfulness is not corrected merely by repeating the word “gift.” The people must let the recognition affect their conduct. They should show gratitude in worship, restraint in consumption, fairness in distribution, and mercy toward those whose access to provision is less secure.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:135

> I truly fear for you the torment of a tremendous day.”

**Hūd's Warning Comes From Fear for Them**

After recalling the gifts given to ʿĀd, Hūd explains why he has urged reverence and obedience. He fears for them because their rejection places them before a tremendous day. The sentence reveals the emotional source of the warning: not envy of their prosperity, but concern about what their choices will bring.

A messenger who sees danger and remains silent would not be trustworthy. Hūd has already stated that he seeks no payment. Here he shows that his motive is protective. He wants the people to escape a consequence that their vanity and violence are making more likely.

The word tremendous gives the coming day a scale that exceeds ordinary human disputes. ʿĀd may think only in rivals, buildings, and present security. Hūd places their lives before a judgment larger than any authority they recognize on earth. Their wealth cannot make that day small.

A serious warning should not be softened into a vague suggestion that every choice has harmless effects. Some actions destroy lives and corrupt communities. God is merciful, but mercy does not make accountability unreal. The fear Hūd expresses protects the people from treating moral consequence as an empty threat.

**A Verified Parallel Shows the Same Warning**

Quran 46:21 describes Hūd warning ʿĀd at the sand-hills and saying that he fears for them a tremendous day's punishment. The parallel confirms that this fear belongs to the heart of his mission. It is not an exaggerated phrase added only after the people's destruction; it is spoken before the outcome is visible.

That passage also situates Hūd among warners who came before and after him. The warning is therefore part of a recurring mercy: God sends guidance before judgment so that a people can recognize its danger while a response remains possible.

ʿĀd's horizon has been narrowed to what it can build, defend, and command. Hūd opens it again. The tremendous day makes present decisions transparent. A monument, a castle, or a victorious attack cannot be considered apart from the account that follows life.

This wider horizon does not make earthly duties irrelevant. It makes them more serious. The way people use provision, treat dependents, and respond to warning will matter beyond the applause of their contemporaries. A society that remembers the final day will not be satisfied with looking successful while harming others.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:136

> They responded, “It is all the same to us whether you warn ˹us˺ or not

**Warnings Are Meant to Preserve Choice**

Hūd warns before the punishment becomes visible. That timing is a mercy because it leaves the people room to change. They treat the warning as irrelevant, but the very fact that it has arrived shows that their choice still matters. A person who can repent does not stand in the same position as one who has already met the consequence.

The answer of ʿĀd tries to reverse this mercy. By saying that warning and no warning are equal, they make opportunity sound pointless. The Qur'anic account shows the opposite: the warning is evidence that God has not abandoned them to their chosen path. Quran 2:6 uses a closely related formulation for people who persist in disbelief, showing that the problem lies in the hearer's refusal, not in any weakness of the warning.

Humility listens even when a message is unwelcome. Arrogance decides in advance that no correction deserves attention. The people of ʿĀd have made their own judgment the measure of every warning, so they can dismiss Hūd without responding to his reasons.

This posture is dangerous in public life. Leaders who treat criticism as irrelevant cannot distinguish between malicious insult and necessary correction. They surround themselves with agreement, then call the resulting confidence proof that no change is needed. Hūd's people show where such confidence can lead.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:137

> This is simply the tradition of our predecessors

**The People Appeal to Precedent**

ʿĀd explains its indifference by appealing to the tradition of its predecessors. The statement shifts responsibility from the present generation to those who came before. What they do feels safe because it is inherited. Yet age and familiarity do not turn a practice into truth, and an ancestor's decision cannot answer for a descendant's judgment.

The appeal is powerful because communities receive language, rituals, social rules, and stories through generations. Inheritance gives people identity, but it can also make examination feel like betrayal. Hūd's call requires ʿĀd to ask whether loyalty to its past has become resistance to God.

The Arabic expression can carry the sense of an inherited way, custom, religion, or claim repeated from earlier people. The ambiguity itself is instructive. Quran 25:5 records a related dismissal in which opponents reduce revelation to tales of the past, showing how an appeal to antiquity can be used to evade the truth being heard now. ʿĀd may be defending its worship, its conduct, or the stories that make its conduct appear respectable. In each case, the appeal to the past is being used as a shield against correction.

A tradition deserves respect when it preserves truth and wisdom, but it does not become authoritative merely by being old. The people must distinguish between memory that helps them obey God and habit that asks God to approve whatever they already do.

**Ancestors Are Not the Final Measure**

Honoring ancestors can include gratitude for their sacrifices and care for their good example. It cannot mean assuming that they were incapable of error. If every predecessor were beyond question, no generation could ever repent of inherited injustice or recover a truth that its elders neglected.

Hūd does not ask ʿĀd to despise its forebears. He asks the living to answer the Lord with their own conscience. The dead cannot carry the responsibility of the present people, and their existence in the past cannot cancel the warning now being delivered.

A practice may survive because it is true, but it may also survive because power protects it, fear repeats it, or people never examine it. Longevity is evidence of transmission, not proof of righteousness. ʿĀd mistakes continuity for confirmation.

This error appears whenever a society says, “We have always done this,” as though history itself were a revelation. The statement avoids the real questions: Does the practice honor God? Does it protect justice? Does it withstand truthful examination? Hūd's message brings those questions back into view.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:138

> And we will never be punished.”

**The Denial Reaches Its Extreme**

ʿĀd's appeal to the past ends with a confident denial of punishment. They do not merely say that they have no evidence of consequence. They say that punishment will never come. The word never reveals a certainty that exceeds their knowledge and turns a hope of escape into a creed.

Their earlier indifference now has a foundation. If no punishment exists, warning can be ignored, inherited custom can govern without review, and power can act without fear. Denial of accountability is not a small theoretical error; it changes the moral atmosphere of a whole society.

The claim can coexist with acknowledgment of a creator while denying that people will be held to account after death. Whether expressed through that distinction or through complete rejection, the practical result is similar: God's claim over conduct is emptied of consequence. Quran 46:26 makes the parallel point that hearing, sight, and understanding do not benefit a people who persist in denying God's signs.

A person may admit that life has a source and still resist the idea that life has a destination and judgment. Hūd's warning joins the two. The Provider who gives cattle, children, gardens, and springs is also the Lord before whom the recipients will answer for their use of those gifts.

**“Never” Converts a Claim Into a Barrier**

To say that punishment will never come closes the door before evidence can be considered. The people are not simply asking for clarification; they are announcing that no future event could correct them. Such language makes repentance appear unnecessary and turns the heart against every warning.

Humility leaves room for correction. It can say, “I do not yet understand,” or, “I need to examine this.” Arrogance says, “This cannot be true,” even when the speaker lacks authority to make that judgment. Hūd's people choose the second posture and make their certainty part of their guilt.

Belief in accountability should protect the rights of people whom worldly power can ignore. If ʿĀd is convinced that no punishment awaits, the suffering of those it attacks can be dismissed as an unfortunate part of dominance. Hūd's warning gives the victim a future before God even when earthly society refuses justice.

That future is not a license for private revenge. It is a reason for the oppressed to trust God's judgment and for the powerful to restrain themselves. The certainty of accountability should make every person more careful with authority, property, speech, and force.

Hūd has identified their Lord, recalled their provision, and expressed fear for their future. The people answer with denial. Their problem is not that no argument was offered. They refuse the framework in which an argument could matter: the framework of revelation, gratitude, and judgment.

A warning can be rejected in many forms, but this is the most complete. They do not merely continue an old practice; they deny the consequence that would make repentance urgent. Their confidence is now directed against the Lord's promise rather than only against Hūd's counsel.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:139

> So they rejected him, and ˹so˺ We destroyed them. Surely in this is a sign. Yet most of them would not believe

**Rejection Becomes a Verdict**

The account now moves from speech to consequence. ʿĀd rejected Hūd, and God destroyed them. The connection is not a claim that every worldly difficulty is a direct punishment that humans can confidently identify. Here the Qur'an itself explains the judgment within a prophetic account: the people had received warning, persisted in rejection, and then met the consequence announced to them.

Their destruction is therefore neither random nor a victory of one tribe over another. It is the outcome of a moral refusal before the Lord. The verse brings the people's confident words into contact with reality. Their claim that punishment would never come does not become true through repetition.

Quran 46:24–25 describes ʿĀd seeing a dense cloud approach their valleys, mistaking it for rain, and then facing a fierce wind whose command destroyed what God willed. It also says that the remains visible were their dwellings. This parallel confirms the seriousness of the present verdict without requiring speculation about every historical ruin.

The scene is morally striking because the people interpret the approaching danger through their expectation of benefit. They see rain where punishment is coming. A community that has trained itself to assume security may misread even a final warning until the opportunity to change has passed.

**The Saved Lesson Is Not a License for Arrogance**

A reader who sees ʿĀd destroyed might feel superior to them. That response would miss the sign. The purpose is not to make later people boast that they would have listened, but to make them examine how they treat warning, custom, strength, and provision.

History can humble the believer or feed another form of pride. The humble reader says, “Let me not repeat their certainty.” He does not assume that belonging to a later community or possessing different technology makes him safe from the pattern exposed here.

The refrain says that most would not believe even after such a sign. This does not mean the event lacked clarity. It shows that people can resist the moral meaning of history. They may treat destruction as an isolated ancient story, blame only unusual circumstances, or admire the remains while ignoring the warning.

The word most also leaves room for those who respond rightly. Truth is not measured by the number of people who accept it. A small group can believe, repent, and be saved from the spiritual pattern even when a dominant society refuses to change.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:140

> And your Lord is certainly the Almighty, Most Merciful

**The Refrain Interprets the Destruction**

After stating that ʿĀd were destroyed, the verse closes with the names of the Lord: the Almighty, Most Merciful. The judgment is therefore not a chaotic disaster outside divine wisdom. It occurs under the power of the One who sent warning and made repentance possible. The names tell the reader how to understand both the severity and the care in the account.

Power explains why ʿĀd's castles, attacks, and confidence could not protect them. Mercy explains why Hūd came to them as a brother, why he asked for no payment, and why he continued to warn them before the final consequence. Quran 46:24–25 likewise places the approaching punishment after Hūd's warning and describes the remains of ʿĀd's dwellings, reinforcing the contrast between divine power and human security. The refrain holds these meanings together.

ʿĀd measured greatness through landmarks, fortifications, physical strength, and the ability to strike others. The title Almighty dismantles that measure. No created power can compete with the Lord who gives existence to people, land, water, and time.

This does not make human strength meaningless. It makes it dependent. A strong society can act beneficially when it knows that its strength is borrowed and accountable. It becomes dangerous when it treats borrowed power as a rival to the One from whom it came.

**Mercy Came Before the Sentence**

The judgment should not be isolated from the warning that preceded it. Hūd did not arrive after the people had been destroyed; he came while they could still respond. His calls to reverence, obedience, gratitude, and restraint were openings of mercy.

Mercy can be present even when a people refuses it. The fact that ʿĀd rejected the warning does not turn the warning into cruelty. It shows that divine care had been offered and that the people chose to close themselves against it. Their destruction confirms the seriousness of that choice.

Some imagine that mercy requires allowing powerful people to continue harming others. The story gives a fuller account. Mercy toward the oppressed and toward the moral order can require ending the power of those who persist in tyranny. The Almighty's judgment protects the truth that violence is not entitled to continue forever.

Yet human beings must not claim God's complete knowledge for themselves. The refrain calls them to oppose injustice with lawful means and humility, not to declare every personal enemy condemned. Divine mercy includes wisdom about hidden circumstances that no human tribunal fully has.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:141

> The people of Thamûd rejected the messengers

**Thamûd Is Introduced by Its Response**

The account of Thamûd begins with a verdict about how the people received revelation: they rejected the messengers. The verse does not first praise their settlements, skill, or resources. It puts their moral response before their material description. A people may be remembered for what it builds, but the Qur'an asks first whether it accepted the truth sent to it.

This opening also connects Thamûd to the communities already mentioned. Noah's people and ʿĀd had heard warning and refused it. Thamûd now enters the same sequence, not because every historical detail is identical, but because human pride keeps returning to the same decision. The names change; the test of submission remains.

**The Community Has a History, Not an Excuse**

Thamûd is a named people with its own memory and way of life. That specificity matters. Revelation does not condemn an imaginary type called “the arrogant”; it addresses a real community whose habits can be examined. Naming the people makes the warning concrete and prevents the reader from treating rejection as a vague flaw found only in legends.

At the same time, a historical name does not place the story safely in the past. Every community develops reasons for trusting its own customs and achievements. Thamûd becomes a mirror when a later society recognizes the same instinct in itself: defend inherited confidence first, and ask about God's command only afterward.

**Rejection Comes Before the Famous Sign**

The verse records the response before the narrative describes the she-camel. This order matters. The people do not begin with an innocent search for evidence and then reluctantly reject an unmistakable sign. Their basic stance toward revelation is already resistant. The later sign exposes that stance; it does not create it from nothing.

Quran 7:73 likewise presents Ṣāliḥ as Thamûd's brother and describes the she-camel as a sign from their Lord. That parallel shows that the miracle was placed within a prior call to worship Allah. The community's central problem was not a shortage of marvels but a refusal to let evidence govern its allegiance.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:142

> when their brother Ṣâliḥ said to them, “Will you not fear ˹Allah˺

**The Messenger Is Close Enough to Be Known**

A local messenger carries a special kind of evidence: the people can compare his claim with the character they have observed. Ṣāliḥ is not asking them to trust an unknown adventurer. They know his family and his conduct. If they reject his warning, they must explain why the message is false rather than simply inventing a reason to distrust his motives.

Quran 7:73 also presents him as Thamûd's brother and describes his call to worship Allah alone. The parallel confirms that the family language is part of the mission's meaning. God sends guidance through a human being who can speak plainly to a human community, not through a voice detached from ordinary life.

Some people hear religious fear as a demand for constant anxiety. Ṣāliḥ's question has a different purpose. Fear of Allah restrains the hand before it harms, the ruler before he abuses authority, and the wealthy person before he turns provision into domination. It is a moral guard placed before the deed, not only a reaction afterward.

This is why the question is addressed to a people known for prosperity. Abundance can reduce a sense of dependence and make restraint seem unnecessary. Taqwa reminds Thamûd that every advantage remains under the Lord's judgment. The secure person is not the one who feels untouchable, but the one who remembers that God sees how security is used.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:143

> I am truly a trustworthy messenger to you

**Trustworthiness Supports a Difficult Claim**

Ṣāliḥ identifies himself as a trustworthy messenger because the people are being asked to accept a message that will unsettle their worship and confidence. Trust is not decorative praise. It addresses the question of whether the speaker is delivering God's command faithfully or using religion to pursue influence, wealth, or revenge.

A community can test a messenger's character through the life it has seen. Ṣāliḥ has grown among Thamûd, so his statement invites them to compare his conduct with his claim. The message should not be dismissed by assigning him a hidden motive without evidence. They must engage what he says and why it follows from God's authority.

The word messenger places Ṣāliḥ in a precise position. He is neither an ordinary adviser speaking only from personal experience nor a divine being who has independent power. He receives and conveys guidance. His trustworthiness includes honesty about the source of his authority and humility about his own place before God.

That balance protects the people from two mistakes. They must not reject the message because Ṣāliḥ is human, and they must not turn Ṣāliḥ into an object of worship because he is a messenger. A reliable envoy points beyond himself. His success is measured by whether people obey the Lord he represents.

**The People Are Not Asked for Blind Admiration**

Trustworthy speech deserves a fair hearing, not a personality cult. Ṣāliḥ does not ask Thamûd to abandon understanding. His claim can be considered alongside the clarity of his call, the coherence of his worship, and the signs God provides. The messenger's character opens the door to listening; it does not replace the truth of what he conveys.

This protects religious life from manipulation. A charismatic speaker can be persuasive while being false, and an honest person can be unpolished while speaking truth. Ṣāliḥ's trustworthiness matters together with his message. The people should neither follow appearances alone nor use the human form of the messenger as an excuse to reject him.

The objection that Ṣāliḥ is one of them will appear explicitly later. This verse answers it in advance by presenting ordinary human familiarity as part of the proof. A messenger who shares the people's world can explain their habits and address their excuses in a language they understand.

Quran 18:110 states the same basic relation for the Prophet Muhammad: the messenger is a human being who receives revelation and calls to one God. Humanity is not a defect in an envoy. It is the condition that makes imitation, instruction, and moral accountability possible for those who hear him.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:144

> So fear Allah, and obey me

**Trust Must Become Obedience**

Ṣāliḥ has said that he is a trustworthy messenger. This verse states what the people should do with that claim: fear Allah and obey the messenger. The command does not ask Thamûd merely to respect Ṣāliḥ's honesty. It asks the community to let his guidance govern its choices.

The order keeps the messenger's role clear. Reverence belongs first to Allah, while obedience to Ṣāliḥ follows because he conveys God's command. The people do not replace direct worship of the Lord with attachment to a human leader. They follow the messenger as an act of submission to the One who sent him.

Fear of Allah gives obedience its right motive. A person may comply with a leader because of force, custom, or the promise of reward. Taqwa is different. It makes the servant aware that God sees the hidden intention and the public action. Obedience then grows from reverence rather than from fear of social consequences alone.

This inner foundation matters for Thamûd because its outward prosperity can hide inward independence. The people may obey one another's customs while refusing the Lord. Ṣāliḥ calls them to a deeper loyalty, one that remains present even when no human authority is watching.

**A Repeated Prophetic Formula Carries Continuity**

The same call was directed to Noah and Hūd in the preceding accounts. Their peoples heard a combination of reverence and obedience because the core of prophetic guidance is stable. Thamûd cannot claim that Ṣāliḥ has invented a new demand simply because it challenges its own practices.

Quran 7:73 presents Ṣāliḥ calling Thamûd to worship Allah alone and identifying a clear proof from their Lord. That parallel shows the command is attached to a call to divine oneness, not to blind loyalty. Obedience to Ṣāliḥ is meaningful because it leads the people away from idols and back to God.

To follow Ṣāliḥ means more than attending to his speech. It includes stopping when the messenger identifies a boundary. Later, the community will face a test involving a protected sign and a shared resource. The present command establishes the principle before that test arrives: God's instruction must control appetite and power.

This is a general measure of obedience. A person who claims to follow revelation but refuses every limit has followed his desire instead. Taqwa becomes visible when a person can leave something alone even though he has the ability to take it.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:145

> I do not ask you for any reward for this ˹message˺. My reward is only from the Lord of all worlds

**A Parallel From Hūd Clarifies the Pattern**

Quran 11:51 records Hūd telling ʿĀd that he asks no reward and that his reward is with the One who created him. Ṣāliḥ's statement follows the same prophetic ethic. The repetition establishes that the messengers do not come to create personal empires. They direct all reward and worship back to God.

The parallel also gives Thamûd a comparison it should understand. Another powerful people heard the same refusal of payment and still rejected its messenger. Ṣāliḥ's mission will test whether Thamûd learns from that earlier failure or repeats it under a different name.

After Ṣāliḥ removes the charge of profit, the people are left with the message itself. They can accept God's oneness and submit to His limits, or reject the call because they prefer their established way. His lack of personal demand makes the moral decision clearer, not less demanding.

For the reader, the verse honors service that expects its deepest reward from God. It also warns against judging truth by the wealth or popularity of the person who conveys it. Ṣāliḥ's authority does not arise from what Thamûd can give him. It arises from faithful service to the Lord of all worlds.

The absence of a demand also protects the poor from a second humiliation. They need not prove their worth by funding the messenger, and the wealthy cannot claim that their contribution gives them control over the message. Divine guidance is not a monument financed by patrons. It is a trust announced to every person under the same Lord.

For Ṣāliḥ himself, expecting reward from God steadies the heart when the audience is ungrateful. A servant may feel the pain of rejection without measuring his worth by the reaction. He continues to speak with clarity because the final judgment of his work belongs to the Lord who knows why he spoke and what the people chose. That dependence keeps service sincere even when visible results are small.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:146

> Do you think you will be ˹forever˺ left secure in what you have here

**A Question Challenges Their Security**

Ṣāliḥ turns from the people's identity as a prosperous community to the assumption beneath their prosperity. Do they think they will be left secure in what they have here? The question does not deny that Thamûd has real comforts. It challenges the leap from possession to permanence, from present ease to an expectation that no judgment or loss can reach them.

Security is a blessing when it produces gratitude and responsibility. It becomes dangerous when it persuades a people that its present arrangement is guaranteed. Ṣāliḥ asks Thamûd to examine the belief hidden inside its confidence: not simply “We have been given much,” but “What we have means we cannot be disturbed.”

People rarely announce that they worship security. They reveal it through the decisions they make to preserve comfort at any cost. A community may sacrifice justice, silence correction, or harm the weak because it fears any change to the order that benefits it. Ṣāliḥ's question brings that silent creed into the open.

The word here also narrows the horizon. Thamûd is absorbed in what lies immediately around it: land, homes, produce, and status. The question asks whether the present world is the whole setting of life. If another life and a final account exist, then present security is temporary and every use of it matters.

**Security Does Not Cancel Accountability**

A people may experience long periods without visible punishment. That delay can look like approval, especially when its leaders are effective at protecting their interests. Ṣāliḥ refuses to let the absence of immediate disruption settle the question. God's patience is not a promise that no account will be required.

The question therefore joins comfort to responsibility. Thamûd has been placed among good things, but the people remain servants rather than owners without limit. The more secure a society feels, the more carefully it should ask how that security was gained, who is excluded from it, and whether God is honored through its preservation.

The phrase “what you have here” can make the earth sound like a permanent estate belonging to its occupants. Yet people receive land for a time and leave it to others. Even a strong civilization is a tenant in history. It may cultivate, build, and govern, but it cannot turn a created place into an eternal guarantee.

Quran 7:74 recalls Thamûd as a people established in the land who built palaces on its plains and carved homes in the mountains. The parallel confirms the connection between their material security and their construction. Their settlement was real, but its reality did not make them independent of the Lord who granted it.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:147

> amid gardens and springs

**The Scene of Security Is Abundant**

The phrase completes the setting of Ṣāliḥ's question. Thamûd imagines security amid gardens and springs. Its environment is not described as empty or desperate. Green growth and flowing water create the conditions in which a people can feel settled, nourished, and confident about tomorrow.

That setting makes the warning more searching. The community is not being asked to fear because every visible support has already vanished. It is being asked to remember God while the supports are present. Gratitude during abundance is a form of worship, and forgetting the Giver in abundance can become more dangerous than acknowledging dependence in hardship.

A garden embodies cooperation among soil, water, cultivation, time, and human labor. Its beauty may appear natural after it has been established, but its life depends on conditions that no person controls completely. The sight should therefore awaken humility. People tend what they receive; they do not create the entire order on which growth depends.

Quran 16:10–11 connects rain with water for drinking, grazing, crops, and fruit. That parallel shows how the Qur'an treats cultivated abundance as a sign that invites reflection. Gardens are not only scenery. They reveal dependence and ask whether the people will respond with thanks or with a stronger claim of self-sufficiency.

**Springs Make Provision Feel Permanent**

A spring appears to offer continuity. Water emerges again and again, so a people may begin to regard its flow as guaranteed. Yet a source that serves a community remains within God's creation and command. Its regularity is a mercy, not a contract granting the people permission to ignore the Provider.

Water also creates a moral relationship among neighbors. Those who control a spring can sustain life or use thirst to command others. Fear of Allah requires just management of what people need. A society cannot call itself secure while its comfort depends on preventing others from sharing the conditions of life.

A green landscape encourages attachment. Thamûd may look at its gardens and conclude that its way of life has been endorsed. Ṣāliḥ's question exposes that mistake. Beauty is real, but it is not a verdict that every belief or practice of the people is right.

A garden can be cultivated by honest work or maintained through exploitation. The appearance of abundance does not reveal the whole moral account. Who labored? Who received the harvest? Who was excluded from the water? The setting of security must be examined through justice as well as admiration.

Gardens and springs support families, workers, travelers, and animals. Their benefit naturally extends beyond the person who first claims them. A prosperous community honors the Giver by making provision serve life broadly rather than concentrating it only where power already exists.

This does not abolish private responsibility or ownership. It places both under a higher duty. The holder may manage a resource, but he cannot forget that God sees the needs of those affected by his decisions. Security becomes trustworthy when it includes protection for the vulnerable rather than comfort for the strongest alone.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:148

> and ˹various˺ crops, and palm trees ˹loaded˺ with tender fruit

**Cultivation Shows the Work of Time**

Ṣāliḥ continues describing the abundance around Thamûd through crops and palms carrying tender fruit. The image is agricultural, but its meaning reaches beyond food. A harvest depends on a sequence that cannot be rushed completely: planting, growth, care, ripening, and collection. The people benefit from time ordered by God, not only from their own effort.

This matters to a community tempted by instant confidence. Crops remind it that life develops through dependence and patience. A society can prepare the soil and tend the plants, but it cannot command every condition that makes fruit appear. Its skill operates within a gift.

The palm provides familiar benefits and also shapes the appearance of the land. Its fruit can nourish, while its form and shade can please the eye. The verse allows Thamûd's prosperity to be recognized without turning prosperity into a moral approval of the people.

Quran 16:11 includes date palms among the growth produced through rain, alongside crops and other fruits. The parallel confirms that agriculture is presented as a sign for reflection. The question is not whether the people may enjoy these goods, but whether enjoyment leads them to remember the One who makes them possible.

**Abundance Can Become a Distraction**

Fruit, fields, and palms may fill attention with immediate pleasure. A society can become so occupied with producing and consuming that it forgets why it was given capacity in the first place. The blessing then becomes a curtain hiding the Provider and the coming account.

Fear of Allah does not require refusing food or beauty. It requires that the enjoyment remain subordinate to worship and justice. Eat with gratitude, trade honestly, preserve the land, and do not let a full storehouse persuade you that no answer will be required for how it was filled.

The crops and palms are alive, productive, and apparently secure, yet their condition depends on time and care. They offer a picture of a people whose prosperity is real but conditional. Thamûd is not asked to deny what it sees. It is asked to interpret what it sees correctly.

The right interpretation is neither “we made this alone” nor “wealth must be rejected.” It is “God has provided this, and we will answer for its use.” That answer would turn cultivation into gratitude. Without it, the heaviest harvest can become another reason for the people to imagine themselves beyond correction.

The burdened palm also offers a picture of mature responsibility. A community that has received much must support more than its own appetite. It carries workers, dependents, neighbors, and future generations within the weight of its wealth. The harvest is sound only when the structure beneath it is strong enough to bear those duties without crushing the vulnerable. Such strength is measured by justice, not by the size of the storehouse. A full harvest cannot excuse an empty conscience, and a society is not prosperous if its weakest members are made to carry the cost of its display. Prosperity becomes a proof of gratitude only when it produces protection, fairness, and generous stewardship.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:149

> to carve homes in the mountains with great skill

**Mountain Homes Display Mastery**

Ṣāliḥ's question reaches the most striking expression of Thamûd's skill: homes cut into mountains. The rock itself becomes a building material, and the settlement appears protected by the permanence of the landscape. Such work requires planning, labor, and technical knowledge. The verse recognizes ability while questioning the pride that can grow around it.

A home is a legitimate need, and a secure dwelling can be a mercy. The problem is not shelter. It is the assumption that mastery over stone gives a people mastery over its future. A mountain dwelling may resist weather and enemies, but it cannot make its occupants independent of God or immune to death.

The Arabic description has been read as skilled or nimble, while another reading can carry the sense of exuberance or exultation. The difference affects the shade of the question. Ṣāliḥ may be challenging technical confidence, or the delight and pride with which the people display it. Both meanings converge on the danger of turning ability into self-admiration.

Skill is honorable when it serves life and is joined to gratitude. It becomes spiritually dangerous when the maker begins to love the evidence of his own power more than the Lord who gave him hands, minds, materials, and time. The issue is not whether Thamûd can build, but what its building teaches the people to believe about themselves.

**The Mountain Does Not Guarantee Permanence**

Rock seems more lasting than wood or cloth, and a carved home can make the future look solid. Yet permanence belongs to God alone. The people who inhabit a mountain pass away, and even the mountain remains under the command of its Creator. A durable structure is still a temporary residence for temporary lives.

Quran 89:9 recalls Thamûd as a people who carved rocks in a valley. That parallel confirms that their mountain work was part of their remembered identity. It also places the achievement in a warning about the fate of powerful peoples. A famous technique can survive in memory while its boastful society does not.

A dwelling protects the body, but it can also protect the heart from correction. Those who feel secure behind thick walls may stop noticing their dependence on neighbors, workers, water, and God. The house becomes a private world in which the occupant imagines that outside claims do not apply.

Ṣāliḥ's warning crosses that boundary. No home can conceal the purpose for which it was built or the actions carried out by those inside it. A mountain can hide a doorway from human eyes, but it cannot hide a people from divine knowledge. Security is sound only when it remains joined to accountability.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:150

> So fear Allah, and obey me

**Obedience Is the Practical Form of Reverence**

The command has two parts because inner awareness must produce outward fidelity. Fear of Allah that never changes behavior remains a claim without proof. Obeying Ṣāliḥ means accepting the instruction he brings even when it limits a profitable habit or challenges a custom protected by the powerful.

A community can admire the messenger's sincerity while refusing his command. That split is precisely what the verse prevents. Trust in Ṣāliḥ must be expressed through obedience to God. The people cannot keep the pleasant part of the message and reject the part that disciplines their appetite.

Ṣāliḥ has already made the same appeal, but repetition is not needless. The people have many layers of confidence to surrender. They trust their land, their crops, their houses, and their technical skill. The repeated command gives the warning time to reach each layer rather than allowing the first refusal to end the conversation.

Quran 7:73 presents Ṣāliḥ's call to worship Allah alone alongside the sign granted to Thamûd. That parallel shows why the command returns: the miracle and the material blessings are both meant to direct the people to God. Repetition is patient instruction before it becomes testimony against them.

**The Command Includes a Limit on Pride**

Thamûd may think obedience is a humiliation after the people have demonstrated such skill. Ṣāliḥ reverses that thought. The humiliating condition is to be ruled by appetite, fear the loss of status, and remain unable to admit dependence. Obedience to the Creator is dignity because it frees the servant from those lesser masters.

Taqwa lets a person say, “I was wrong,” even when the admission costs honor. It lets a society change course before its habits become a public disaster. The command is strong because pride is strong, but its purpose is mercy.

Thamûd cannot be governed at once by God's warning and by the assumption that its desires are final. One principle must control the other. Ṣāliḥ asks the people to place revelation above the instincts produced by wealth, architecture, and custom.

That choice appears in small decisions before it appears in a crisis. It is present when a leader accepts correction, when a wealthy person shares, and when a community refuses to exploit a weaker neighbor. Obedience is built through such choices, not announced only when the consequences are visible.

The command closes Ṣāliḥ's first appeal and leaves Thamûd with a clear standard before it responds. Whatever sign or demand comes next, the principle has already been stated. The community's later conduct will reveal whether it heard the command as guidance or as an obstacle.

For the reader, the verse joins faith to stewardship. Reverence is not escape from the world, and material success is not safety from God. Use what He gives, submit the use to His limits, and let obedience remain stronger than the desire to preserve a self-made image.

A community that accepts this command can enjoy what it has without being owned by it. Its fields, homes, and technical gifts become instruments of service. The same abundance that once fed pride can then teach humility, because every successful use of it points back to the Lord who made the blessing possible.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:151

> And do not follow the command of the transgressors

**The Arabic Sense Includes Excess**

The word used for the transgressors is associated with crossing bounds and, in the source discussion, with prodigality or wasteful excess. That range is important. Corruption is not limited to a single violent act. It can include a pattern of wanting more than justice permits and encouraging others to pursue worldly advantage without restraint.

This meaning connects the command to Thamûd's material confidence. The people are surrounded by crops, homes, and security, so leaders who promise ever greater consumption can easily win them. Ṣāliḥ tells them not to let abundance turn into a reason to obey those who have forgotten moderation.

A transgressor may command a following because he knows how to speak to fear and desire. He offers the crowd a sense of belonging and promises that no limit should restrain its strength. Popular agreement does not make the command lawful. A whole society can be carried toward harm when it rewards the loudest appetite.

Following revelation requires the courage to pause before the crowd. Ask what the command does to the weak, the land, the truth, and the worship of God. If it produces humiliation, waste, or aggression, obedience to a leader becomes disobedience to the Lord.

Leadership is not condemned simply because it directs others. A just leader can organize work, protect people, settle disputes, and distribute responsibility. The danger arises when authority is separated from accountability. The transgressors want people to follow the command while ignoring the consequences borne by others.

Ṣāliḥ's warning gives every person a duty to judge the moral direction of an order. “I was only obeying” cannot transform wrongdoing into righteousness. A servant may have limited power, but he still has a conscience and remains responsible for the choices he makes within the limits of his ability.

**Moderation Protects the Community**

Quran 7:31 commands people to eat and drink without waste and says that God does not love the wasteful. That parallel clarifies one dimension of Ṣāliḥ's warning. Excess is not harmless when it consumes resources, teaches vanity, and leaves others to bear scarcity. Moderation is a form of obedience, not a refusal of lawful enjoyment.

A community that practices moderation is harder for transgressors to manipulate. Its people are less willing to defend a ruler's extravagance as their own success. They can recognize that a spectacle funded by injustice is not prosperity in the moral sense.

At first, a person may follow a transgressor for convenience or fear. Repetition then changes the person's sense of normality. What once seemed excessive becomes expected, and resistance begins to look unreasonable. Ṣāliḥ intervenes before the habit becomes the people's identity.

The warning is therefore preventative. Thamûd still has a choice about whom it will imitate and whose commands it will treat as authoritative. A society does not need to wait until corruption fills every institution before refusing its leaders. It can stop following the pattern while correction remains possible.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:152

> who spread corruption throughout the land, never setting things right.”

**Corrupt Leaders Can Call Themselves Reformers**

Those who cause harm rarely describe themselves as corrupt. They may claim to be defending the people, preserving tradition, or bringing progress. Quran 2:11–12 describes a related posture in which people deny that they spread corruption and present themselves as reformers. The parallel shows why self-description cannot be the measure of a leader's work.

Results and methods must be examined. Does the policy protect life or consume it? Does it make truth easier to speak or punish correction? Does it distribute benefit fairly or enrich a narrow circle? A title such as reformer has no value when the actual effect is damage.

The land includes the physical environment, but the phrase also embraces the human order established upon it. Corruption can wound rivers and fields, and it can wound trust, family life, law, and worship. Ṣāliḥ's warning is broad because transgression rarely stays in one area. Disorder in power spreads through the whole community.

Quran 7:56 likewise forbids spreading corruption after the land has been set right and joins the warning to hope and fear of Allah. That parallel connects environmental and social responsibility with worship. A people does not honor God by praying while using its authority to ruin the world around it.

It is easy to identify a corrupt leader and still fail to repair what he damaged. Restoration requires truthful judgment, protection for victims, and patient rebuilding of trust. It may also require changing the systems that rewarded the wrongdoing. Ṣāliḥ's phrase “do not set things right” shows that the failure is active: the transgressors either refuse repair or make it impossible.

A just community must therefore measure leaders by what they restore, not only by what they promise. Apologies without restitution leave the injured carrying the cost. New buildings without honest law leave the underlying corruption intact. Iṣlāḥ joins moral intention to concrete correction.

**Corruption Often Hides Behind Prosperity**

A people can point to gardens, crops, and homes as proof that its leadership works. Material output may distract from the people excluded, exploited, or silenced to produce it. Ṣāliḥ asks Thamûd to look beneath visible success and inspect the relationships that make success possible.

Prosperity is not proof of justice, just as poverty is not proof of guilt. The moral question concerns how resources are gained and used. A community may be rich and well ordered when it protects rights, or rich and corrupt when wealth is built on domination. The land remembers more than the monuments display.

The transgressors lead, but the community is warned not to follow. This means ordinary people are not powerless in the moral sense. They may not control every institution, yet they can refuse praise for injustice, protect one another, and withhold cooperation from harmful commands where they can.

Responsibility should be assigned carefully. A victim is not blamed for the harm done to him, and a powerless person may be unable to resist openly. The verse addresses those who choose to follow and support corruption despite knowing what it does. Their obedience helps a bad order continue.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:153

> They replied, “You are simply bewitched

**The Accusation Can Carry Two Senses**

The Arabic wording allows a discussion about what Thamûd means by the charge. It may refer to a person affected by sorcery, or it may carry a sense connected with a human creature who eats and lives ordinary bodily life. The first reading attacks Ṣāliḥ's reliability; the second treats his humanity as evidence that he cannot be a messenger.

Both readings reveal the same refusal. The people do not ask whether the call to worship God is true. They seek a defect in the messenger that will make obedience unnecessary. The ambiguity does not weaken the verse. It shows how rejection can use either superstition or a narrow view of humanity to protect itself.

Thamûd may think that a messenger should be unlike the people he addresses. The Qur'an repeatedly corrects that expectation. Quran 25:7 records critics objecting that a messenger eats food and walks through marketplaces, while Quran 18:110 presents the messenger as human while distinguishing him through revelation.

Humanity is not proof against prophethood. It is what makes the messenger an example that people can understand and imitate. He experiences hunger, fear, work, and social pressure, yet he remains faithful to God's command. His ordinary needs do not erase the extraordinary trust placed in him.

**The Accusation Cannot Erase Ṣāliḥ's Brotherly Relation**

Ṣāliḥ has spoken as their brother and called them to fear Allah. The people know that he shares their life, yet they treat that closeness as something to overcome with a slur. Their familiarity should have supplied a basis for trust, but pride turns knowledge of the messenger into a reason to belittle him.

A community may remember a person's ordinary past and use it to deny his present calling. That reasoning assumes that God cannot choose someone known to the people. The Qur'anic pattern rejects the assumption. Familiarity can test whether a society follows truth or only honors distant figures.

A name placed on a messenger does not answer his argument. Thamûd still has to face the call to worship God, the demand for taqwa, and the question of how it uses its gifts. The accusation does not touch those matters. It only tries to make the speaker disappear behind a word.

The reader should therefore learn to separate diagnosis from dismissal. Sometimes a person can be genuinely ill or deceived, but such a claim requires evidence and careful judgment. It cannot be assumed whenever a truthful warning is uncomfortable. Ṣāliḥ's opponents provide no proof; they provide a shield for their refusal.

The charge also reveals how quickly a community can make an insult sound like a conclusion. Repeated often enough, the label may become part of public memory and obscure the messenger's actual words. Truthful judgment requires returning to the claim itself rather than accepting the crowd's description of the person who made it.

The people speak with confidence, but confidence is not the same as knowledge. Their accusation may make the crowd feel secure, yet it leaves the central question unanswered. What does God require of them, and is Ṣāliḥ conveying it faithfully?

A believer protects the heart from this form of certainty by remaining willing to examine. Do not make the messenger's humanity, unfamiliarity, or social position a substitute for testing the message. Thamûd's word “bewitched” is short, but it displays a long refusal to let truth judge the people who hear it.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:154

> You are only a human being like us, so bring forth a sign if what you say is true.”

**Humanity Becomes Their Objection**

Thamûd expands its accusation by saying that Ṣāliḥ is a human being like them. The phrase sounds reasonable until its implication is examined. They are not only observing that the messenger shares human needs. They are using shared humanity to deny that God can choose a human envoy.

The Qur'an presents the objection as a recurring one.
