Quran 25:7 records critics objecting to a messenger's ordinary eating and movement among people, while Quran 18:110 affirms human likeness together with revelation and divine oneness. The pattern shows that humanity is not the contradiction Thamûd imagines. The contradiction lies in expecting guidance while refusing the form God gives it.

If Ṣāliḥ were an angel, Thamûd could claim that obedience was impossible for ordinary people. A human messenger removes that excuse. He lives within the same limits of bodily life, yet he demonstrates what it means to submit to God. His humanity makes the path concrete rather than remote.

The people do not need to imitate an otherworldly creature. They can hear a person who knows hunger, work, family, fear, and social pressure. The messenger's example tells them that taqwa is not reserved for a different kind of being. It is a real obligation for people like themselves.

**The Demand for a Sign Is Conditional**

They ask for a sign if Ṣāliḥ is truthful. A request for evidence can be sincere, but this wording is placed after their accusation and rejection. It sounds less like an open investigation than a challenge designed to control the terms of belief. They will decide what counts as proof before considering the message.

A sign cannot replace willingness to obey. Someone determined to resist may demand another sign after receiving one, not because evidence is absent but because submission is unwelcome. Ṣāliḥ's earlier call already has moral clarity; the requested sign will test whether the people recognize God's authority or treat a miracle as a spectacle they command.

A prophet does not manufacture signs to prove personal strength. They are acts of God that support the truth of the messenger's call. This distinction prevents the messenger from becoming a magician who controls extraordinary events for applause. Ṣāliḥ's role is to convey and explain, while God decides what sign will appear.

Quran 7:73 presents the she-camel as a clear proof from the Lord and calls it a sign for Thamûd. That parallel answers the demand in the present verse. The sign is not a performance by Ṣāliḥ; it is a divine test placed before the people.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:155

> Ṣâliḥ said, “Here is a camel. She will have her turn to drink as you have yours, each on an appointed day

**The Sign Arrives as a Living Trust**

Ṣāliḥ answers Thamûd's demand for proof by identifying a she-camel as a sign from God. The sign is not only an object to admire. It is a living creature placed within the people's daily economy and given a protected share of water. The miracle therefore enters the ordinary world where the community's habits and interests operate.

Quran 7:73 also calls the she-camel a clear proof from the Lord and instructs Thamûd to leave her to graze. The parallel makes the source of the sign clear. Ṣāliḥ presents it, but he does not claim to have created or controlled it. The camel points beyond the messenger to the Lord whose authority is being tested.

The verse assigns a turn of drinking to the camel and a turn to the people. The arrangement is not an invitation to chaos or an abolition of human need. It establishes a boundary within shared provision. A creature that has become a divine sign must not be treated as an ordinary possession to be pushed aside whenever the powerful want more.

The schedule also makes justice visible. Water is limited, and limited resources expose whether a community can accept restraint. Thamûd is asked to live with an allocation that acknowledges another creature's claim. The test is not only belief in a miracle; it is willingness to let belief change behavior.

The source discussion preserves two readings of the appointed arrangement. It may mean that the camel has a designated day and the people have another, or that each receives an assigned share of the water. The exact scheduling detail is less important than the moral structure: access is regulated, and neither side may erase the other's right.

This interpretive range does not weaken the command. Both readings make the resource a trust rather than a free-for-all. Thamûd must accept a limit that its appetite did not design. The divine sign turns water use into an act of obedience and exposes the community's capacity for fairness.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:156

> And do not ever touch her with harm, or you will be overtaken by the torment of a tremendous day.”

**The Camel's Protection Is a Test of Obedience**

The animal is not protected because it can argue its case or defend itself. It is protected because God has commanded the people to respect it. That fact exposes the difference between justice and power. If Thamûd honors only the rights of those who can demand them, its system is not just.

Quran 11:64 similarly describes the she-camel as a sign and warns the people not to harm her. The parallel confirms that the prohibition belongs to the central meaning of the event. The miracle tests whether the community will accept a divine allocation even when its own desire would prefer unrestricted access.

Thamûd may experience the command as a restriction on its freedom. In reality, the limit protects the community from the consequences of unrestrained competition. A schedule for water and a ban on harm create room for peaceful coexistence. The boundary saves the people from allowing scarcity to become a reason for violence.

This is a general feature of divine law. A limit may frustrate immediate appetite while preserving life, dignity, and future benefit. Ṣāliḥ's warning asks the people to trust that justice is not measured by how much one can seize in a single moment.

**Collective Knowledge Increases Responsibility**

The prohibition is public. Thamûd hears it as a community, and the assigned arrangement is known among the people. No one can fairly say that the act was an accident of ignorance after the boundary has been announced. Those who encourage harm, those who assist it, and those who celebrate it all face questions about their part.

Individual responsibility remains real. A crowd cannot make a wrong act right, and a leader cannot carry every person's intention. But a society also has a collective culture that either protects limits or makes violation easy. Ṣāliḥ warns the whole people because the whole people must decide what it will permit.

The word ever gives urgency to the command. Do not begin the harm, repeat it, or treat it as a small exception. A person who has not yet violated the boundary can still obey. Even a community that has begun to resent the sign can step back before its resentment becomes irreversible action.

The warning is severe because the opportunity is still open. God tells Thamûd the consequence in advance so that it can choose safety through restraint. The wise response is not to test how close one may come to disobedience, but to honor the boundary as soon as it is known.

The particular camel belongs to the history of Thamûd, but the principle remains readable. God's commands can place limits around people, animals, resources, and places. A believer does not ask only what he can take; he asks what God has forbidden him to harm.

The sign becomes a lesson when it changes the hand before the hand strikes. Fear the tremendous day by respecting the small boundary today. Ṣāliḥ's warning joins compassion, justice, and worship: leave what God has protected, and do not let appetite persuade you that the command is negotiable.

A protected creature also teaches the community how to handle power when no one can force it to be kind. Mercy is not weakness here. It is disciplined strength, choosing not to use capacity for harm because God has placed a limit around the vulnerable.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:157

> But they killed her, becoming regretful

**The People Cross the Boundary**

Thamûd does what it was warned not to do: it kills the she-camel. The act is brief in the verse, but it carries the weight of the earlier call, the public sign, and the assigned share of water. The people are not acting without guidance. They cross a boundary that Ṣāliḥ has explained and connected to a tremendous consequence.

The killing is therefore more than damage to an animal. It is a declaration that the people's desire has priority over God's command. They have received a sign and a limit, yet they choose to remove the sign rather than reform their appetite.

The verse speaks of the people in the plural even though an act of killing is carried out through particular hands. The wording holds the community responsible for the direction it accepted and the culture that permitted the violation. A society cannot celebrate the outcome while pretending that only one isolated person expressed its will.

This does not erase individual accountability. The one who acts, the one who commands, the one who assists, and the one who knowingly encourages may bear different responsibilities. The larger lesson is that collective approval can make a single violent act possible. Thamûd's rejection is expressed through the community's relation to the deed.

**Remorse Arrives After the Choice**

The people become regretful, but the placement of the remorse is significant. They regret after crossing the boundary, not before. Their feeling does not turn the act into obedience or restore the sign they destroyed. Regret can be morally valuable, but it cannot be used as proof that the original decision was harmless.

There is a difference between remorse that leads to honest repentance and regret that merely mourns the loss of comfort. Thamûd may fear what its act will bring, but fear of consequence is not yet the same as submission to God. True repentance names the wrong, abandons its cause, and seeks to repair what can be repaired.

The people had challenged Ṣāliḥ's claim and demanded proof. God gave them a living sign with a clear instruction. Their treatment of the camel shows that their problem was not simply a lack of evidence. They reject the sign by acting against the command attached to it.

Quran 91:13–14 links the warning about the camel's turn to drink with the people's later slaughter of her and the punishment that followed. The parallel confirms the sequence: command, violation, and consequence. It also shows why the remorse cannot be separated from the earlier defiance.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:158

> So the punishment overtook them. Surely in this is a sign. Yet most of them would not believe

**The Consequence Arrives After the Warning**

The punishment overtakes Thamûd after the people have rejected Ṣāliḥ's call and killed the she-camel. The wording presents the judgment as an arrival that catches the community, not because God lacked knowledge of their conduct, but because the people had lived as though consequence could never reach them.

The event must be read within the sequence. Warning came first, a sign was given, a limit was announced, and the people chose violation. The punishment is not an unexplained disaster placed beside the story. It confirms the seriousness of the command the people treated as negotiable.

The verse calls what happened a sign. The sign is not only the camel or the extraordinary event. It includes the judgment that followed the community's defiance. God has shown the people a proof, and then shown what happens when a people treats proof as an obstacle to be destroyed.

Quran 7:77–78 describes the people killing the camel, defying their Lord's command, and then being struck by an overwhelming earthquake. The parallel gives the punishment a concrete form while preserving the moral center: the event is tied to rebellion, not to an arbitrary loss of fortune.

**The Punishment Overtakes False Security**

Thamûd had imagined itself secure among gardens, crops, palms, and mountain homes. The verb overtook cuts through that confidence. A community can plan for decades and still be unable to schedule the moment when its security ends. Created arrangements remain subject to the command of the Creator.

This does not mean that every sudden disaster can be confidently assigned by human beings to a particular sin. The Qur'an identifies the judgment here because revelation discloses the case. Later observers must learn humility rather than turn the verse into permission to condemn every victim of calamity.

Thamûd's strength could carve mountains and manage resources, but it could not protect the people from the consequence of defying God. The contrast between their skill and their end is part of the sign. Ability is real, yet it is never ultimate.

A society may have technology, wealth, or strong institutions and still be morally fragile. If its order depends on injustice, the appearance of stability conceals a weakness. The true strength of a community includes willingness to accept correction and restrain appetite before God forces the truth upon it.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:159

> And your Lord is certainly the Almighty, Most Merciful

**The Two Names Reject Two Distortions**

One distortion sees only power and imagines God as a force whose judgment leaves no hope. Another sees only mercy and assumes that no wrongdoing can bring a final consequence. The refrain rejects both. God is Mighty enough to judge and Merciful enough to warn, delay, and accept a sincere return.

A believer needs both truths. Fear of the Almighty prevents pride, while hope in the Merciful prevents despair. Thamûd's story especially corrects presumption, the idea that abundance and delay prove safety. The mercy they received was an invitation to repent, not permission to continue violating the command.

The final names direct attention away from the spectacle of a ruined people. A reader who delights in the punishment has not understood the Most Merciful. The event should produce sober recognition that justice is real and that God's care includes protecting the truth from being erased by powerful refusal.

This also disciplines human judgment. People can resist oppression and seek lawful justice, but they should not claim to know every hidden reason behind a calamity. God's power is complete because His knowledge is complete. Human beings must act with evidence, restraint, and humility.

Quran 7:77–78 records the killing of the camel followed by an overwhelming earthquake that left the people lifeless in their homes. That parallel confirms the severity of the judgment described here. It also preserves the order that makes the judgment intelligible: a clear sign and command came before the community's defiance.

The refrain therefore keeps the parallel from becoming a mere disaster report. The Lord's power is shown in the punishment, and His mercy is shown in the warning and sign that preceded it. The event is morally legible because both names remain present.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:160

> The people of Lot rejected the messengers

**Lot's Message Is Not a Private Innovation**

The plural messengers places Lot within continuity. His call must be heard alongside the prophetic summons already described in this sūrah: worship God, fear Him, obey the messenger, and do not let appetite rule the community. The setting changes, but divine oneness and moral accountability remain central.

Quran 7:80 introduces Lot's rebuke to his people within a broader sequence of prophetic warnings. That parallel confirms that the narrative is not inviting curiosity about an isolated scandal. It is exposing what happens when a society is warned about wrongdoing and chooses to defend the wrongdoing instead.

A reader may become accustomed to the refrain and stop hearing it. Repetition can dull attention, but it can also reveal that human beings repeatedly need the same correction. The verse insists that a new society is not exempt merely because another society has already failed.

Each community has its own reasons for believing it is different. It may point to its culture, its intelligence, or its freedoms. The repeated verdict asks a more serious question: when a truthful messenger challenges the community, will it submit to God or make its own preference the measure of truth?

**Collective Identity Cannot Carry a Person to Safety**

Being associated with a people does not determine a person's final standing. The community may reject while an individual believes, and a person may belong outwardly to a group without sharing every intention within it. The verse names the collective because collective choices matter, but God's judgment does not erase individual accountability.

This distinction prevents both contempt and complacency. Do not despise every person because of a group's rejection, and do not assume that belonging to a group protects you from its moral direction. Each hearer must decide whether to support falsehood, remain silent before it, or follow the guidance God sends.

The verse is a threshold. It does not yet detail every claim made by Lot or every response of his people; it establishes the fact that rejection governs the story. The reader should carry forward the earlier lessons: prosperity, custom, and majority opinion cannot replace submission to God.

A messenger's presence is mercy because it gives the community an opportunity to change before judgment. If the people reject that mercy, the outcome will not be a mystery of fate. Their own response will reveal what they chose to protect. The opening sentence asks the reader to listen before the next exchange begins.

The phrase “the people of Lot” can tempt later readers to look at another society with distance. The Qur'an places the verse here to prevent that escape. Every community has desires it protects and boundaries it resents. The question is whether it will accept correction when revelation confronts those desires.

The safest response is humility. Hear the messenger, test claims by God's guidance, and do not treat a familiar social order as an authority above Him. The people of Lot rejected the messengers; the account is a warning to anyone who believes that a community's insistence can make its chosen way immune to judgment.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:161

> when their brother Lot said to them, “Will you not fear ˹Allah˺

**A Local Messenger Removes an Excuse**

The people cannot claim that Lot knows nothing of their circumstances. He belongs to them and can speak their language. The closeness makes their response more responsible: they are hearing a warning from someone who has shared their environment and understands the social pressure around the conduct he addresses.

Quran 7:80 also introduces Lot's rebuke to his people in the context of a shameful practice. The parallel confirms that the question is a moral summons, not a private dispute between a prophet and a few individuals. Lot is calling the community to examine its public direction before harm becomes normalized.

A distant rule can be discussed abstractly. Lot's question is carried by a person whose relationship to the community can be felt. He has family ties, memories, and a stake in what happens. His warning is not delivered from superiority; it comes from concern for people with whom he is connected.

That human closeness also makes rejection more revealing. The people know that Lot is not attacking them because he is an outsider who hates them. They reject a moral claim because it threatens what they have chosen to protect. Their resistance cannot be explained simply as a clash of unfamiliar cultures.

Sexual desire, social custom, and public approval can reinforce one another. A person may participate because he wants the act, because others praise it, or because he fears being excluded if he objects. Lot's question places every one of those pressures beneath God. The crowd is not allowed to turn agreement into moral truth.

A society that fears Allah must protect the ability to refuse wrongdoing. It cannot force every person to celebrate what he believes is forbidden, nor can it make public approval a substitute for conscience. Taqwa creates space for truthful correction even when the dominant mood is hostile.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:162

> I am truly a trustworthy messenger to you

**A Messenger From Among the People Can Be Known**

Lot's closeness to the community is part of the moral situation. The people have seen his conduct, family life, and manner of dealing with others. They cannot say that the messenger appeared without a history. If they disagree, they must address the message rather than pretend that Lot's character is unknowable.

Familiarity, however, can be used in the opposite way. People may say that they know Lot too well to accept a special calling from him. They mistake ordinary human familiarity for evidence against revelation. A messenger's shared humanity makes his example accessible; it does not cancel the trust he carries.

A messenger who wants popularity would avoid the subject that threatens the community's pride. Lot does not do that. He speaks about conduct the people have normalized and asks them to fear God. His courage is not a desire to humiliate them. It is the cost of being faithful to a warning that could save them.

The trustworthy person tells the truth even when silence would be easier. He does not soften the command until it loses its meaning, and he does not sharpen it with insults to satisfy anger. Lot's position shows that moral clarity and compassion can inhabit the same speech.

**The Messenger Does Not Own the Message**

A false religious leader may treat a message as personal property, demanding loyalty to himself and using revelation to build an empire. Lot's wording resists that pattern. He is a messenger, which means that the truth precedes him and continues beyond him. The people must follow God, not Lot's ego.

This also means that Lot cannot bargain away the difficult part of the warning. He is not free to edit divine guidance because his audience is powerful or because rejection is painful. Trustworthiness is measured by fidelity to the Lord, not by the comfort his words create.

Lot remains a human being with fear, grief, and limited control over the people. Trustworthiness does not mean possessing every power or knowing every hidden event. It means conveying what God has given him and acting honestly within his duty.

Quran 18:110 presents the same principle for the Prophet Muhammad: human nature and revelation are not opposites. A messenger's humanity shows dependence on God, while the revelation he receives gives him an authority no personal invention could supply. Lot's people must distinguish the messenger's human form from the divine source of his call.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:163

> So fear Allah, and obey me

**The Messenger's Obedience Is the Pattern**

Lot does not stand outside the command he gives. He fears Allah and obeys the Lord by delivering a difficult warning. His authority is not based on claiming that he is above human moral responsibility. He is the first servant of the message he brings.

This makes prophetic obedience visible. The messenger's life should direct people toward God rather than toward a private code reserved for others. Lot's call is credible because it is a call to submission, not an opportunity for him to enjoy control over the community.

The same wording has appeared in the calls of Noah, Hūd, and Ṣāliḥ. The repetition shows that the moral center of prophecy does not change when the social problem changes. Worship God, fear Him, and obey the messenger. A community cannot declare that its custom is exempt because its temptation has a different form.

Quran 29:36 records Shuʿayb calling Midian to worship Allah, hope for the Last Day, and avoid corruption. The parallel shows how reverence for God becomes a public ethic. Fear is not an escape from society; it is what keeps society from turning desire and power into corruption.

**Obedience Does Not Mean Human Domination**

Because Lot says “obey me,” the people might imagine that he seeks personal rule. The first part of the sentence prevents that reading. Obedience is directed through him to Allah. A true messenger does not make the people dependent on his personality or claim a right to be worshipped.

The distinction is important for every community. Religious obedience must be measured by the revealed call, not by the unchecked preference of a leader. Lot's mission curbs human domination; it does not establish a new form of it.

Lot could have pronounced the people hopeless, but he calls them to fear and obey. The command is an opportunity to stop defending a harmful way of life and return to God. Warning is severe because the danger is real, yet it remains merciful while repentance is possible.

The reader should hear the sentence as more than a refrain. It asks whether divine guidance is allowed to govern desire, speech, and public pressure. Taqwa is not an abstract feeling. It is the courage to obey God when the surrounding culture insists that obedience is unnecessary.

The phrase also teaches the messenger's community how to resist coercion. If a leader commands people to praise what God forbids, refusing that command is not rebellion against truth. It is loyalty to the higher authority. Such refusal should remain principled rather than abusive, because the servant who fears Allah must not become another source of domination.

Lot's call therefore joins personal restraint with public responsibility. The person disciplines his own desire, and the community refuses to build laws, celebrations, or pressures around desire's unchecked rule. Obedience begins in the heart but becomes visible in the moral shape a people agrees to protect. It asks the community to make reverence stronger than pressure, fashion, and the fear of standing alone. A community cannot call itself obedient while punishing every person who honors God's boundary.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:164

> I do not ask you for any reward for this ˹message˺. My reward is only from the Lord of all worlds

**Lot Denies a Financial Motive**

Lot has asked his people to fear Allah and obey him, a request that challenges a settled public practice. They could accuse him of seeking money, influence, or a position above the community. He answers that he asks no reward from them. His warning is not a transaction in which moral approval can be bought.

That independence matters because a paid advocate may be tempted to protect the interests of the person who funds him. Lot places his reward with God, so the community cannot purchase his silence or demand a softer version of the message. His concern for them is real, but it is not a strategy for personal enrichment.

Lot names God as the Lord of all worlds rather than as a tribal patron. The authority and recompense behind his mission extend beyond the people in front of him. Their approval is limited, their wealth is temporary, and their power cannot determine the value of faithful service.

This title also means that Lot's message is not an attempt to impose one family's private code on a rival group. He speaks for the Lord of every people. The community is being asked to answer to a universal Creator, not to submit to Lot because he wants to win a local dispute.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:165

> Why do you ˹men˺ lust after fellow men

**Desire Does Not Become Right Through Intensity**

The force of an appetite can make it feel authoritative. A person may reason that because a desire is strong, fulfilling it must be necessary or good. Lot's question exposes the gap between intensity and moral truth. Feelings can be real without becoming commands that God has authorized.

Taqwa gives the servant a place to stand when desire presses hard. It does not require pretending that temptation is absent. It requires recognizing that the self is not its own lawgiver. A desire must be disciplined by revelation, wisdom, and concern for the consequences imposed on oneself and others.

Quran 29:28–30 records Lot rebuking his people for male-male desire and also mentions their abuse of travelers and misconduct in public gatherings. That parallel shows that the present question belongs to a wider moral disorder. The problem is not presented as an isolated private feeling but as part of a community's public conduct.

The parallel also explains why Lot's warning carries social urgency. When a practice is reinforced by intimidation, public celebration, or harm to outsiders, it affects more than the people who choose it. A community has to examine the culture it creates around desire and power.

The verse condemns a practice without instructing believers to treat every person associated with it as worthless. A sinner remains a human being who can repent and whose basic rights must not be violated. Lot's role is to warn and invite return, not to authorize humiliation or violence.

This distinction is essential because moral correction can be corrupted by hatred. A person who despises a forbidden act may still behave unjustly toward the one who commits it. Fear of Allah requires the critic to obey God's limits while calling others back to them.

Lot speaks to men as a group because the conduct has become a defining part of the society he addresses. A private struggle can be met with counsel and repentance; a public order that pressures people to accept wrongdoing requires a public warning. The prophet challenges the standard the community is building around desire.

A culture can make resistance difficult by mocking those who refuse, excluding families who seek guidance, or teaching the young that restraint is shameful. Lot's question interrupts that formation. It tells the people that a society does not have the right to redefine God's command simply because it has organized itself around disobedience.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:166

> leaving the wives that your Lord has created for you? In fact, you are a transgressing people.”

**The People Are Called Transgressors**

Lot does not describe the conduct as an innocent variation that becomes right through social approval. He calls the people transgressors, meaning that they have crossed a boundary. The term places their behavior within a larger pattern of refusing God's limits, not merely within a debate over taste.

Transgression also includes the social harms described in the parallel account. Quran 29:29 associates the people with abuse of travelers and public immorality alongside their sexual wrongdoing. That context shows why the community's condition is not a private matter detached from justice. A people that celebrates desire while violating others has crossed several boundaries at once.

Calling a people transgressors does not erase their human status. The warning leaves room for repentance, and the messenger's concern shows that he wants them rescued rather than humiliated. A believer can say that an act is forbidden while refusing to mock, assault, expose, or dehumanize the person who commits it.

This discipline is especially important when a society is polarized. Moral truth should not be weakened to avoid offense, but the delivery of truth must not become an excuse for cruelty. Lot's sentence is severe because the matter is severe, yet its purpose is to stop the community and return it to God.

The mention of wives can also remind the reader that lawful sexuality is not only about a permitted object. Marriage carries obligations toward another person. It requires consent, faithfulness, care, and responsibility for consequences. A person cannot claim to honor God's design while using a spouse for appetite and neglecting her rights.

That broader responsibility keeps the verse from being reduced to a slogan. The lawful path is morally meaningful because it is ordered, relational, and accountable. Lot challenges the people to leave transgression, not simply to exchange one form of selfishness for another.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:167

> They threatened, “If you do not desist, O Lot, you will surely be expelled.”

**The Messenger's Place Is Not Granted by the Crowd**

Lot belongs to the community by human relation, but his mission comes from God. The people may control access to their town, yet they cannot decide whether God's command is true by voting on it. Their threat can endanger Lot's worldly residence, but it cannot cancel his status as a messenger.

This distinction gives courage to anyone who loses social approval for obeying God. Belonging to a place is valuable, and expulsion is painful, but no community is the final owner of a servant's conscience. The believer seeks to remain just and peaceful, yet he cannot purchase membership by surrendering truth.

The people behave as if Lot's warning is the shameful act and their practice is the norm that must be protected. Their response reverses the moral positions. Instead of examining themselves, they label the corrector intolerable. This is a common feature of entrenched wrongdoing: correction is experienced as aggression because it threatens comfort.

The Qur'an does not ask the reader to accept every accusation made by a dissenter. Truthful correction requires knowledge, wisdom, and justice. In Lot's case, the surrounding verses establish his trustworthiness and the revealed nature of his warning. The threat therefore confirms resistance; it does not establish the people's innocence.

Quran 7:82 records Lot's people responding to his warning by saying that they should be expelled from the town. That parallel confirms that expulsion was not an isolated metaphor but a stated social response to Lot's moral opposition.

The repetition across the accounts shows the cost of challenging a community's identity. When wrongdoing becomes a badge of belonging, the people may prefer to remove the truthful person rather than reconsider the practice. Lot's faithfulness is tested not only by the content of his warning but by the pressure to leave.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:168

> Lot responded, “I am truly one of those who despise your ˹shameful˺ practice

**The Messenger Will Not Bargain Away Truth**

The people have offered Lot a choice between silence and expulsion. His reply shows that the threat has not made him uncertain about the command. He will not call shameful what God has forbidden merely to preserve social comfort.

This is an important test of prophetic integrity. A messenger who changes his moral judgment whenever the crowd threatens him is not trustworthy. Lot's firmness does not arise from confidence in his own strength. It arises from knowing that the Lord's approval matters more than the community's permission to remain.

Quran 29:28–30 describes Lot's rebuke of the men and also mentions their abuse of travelers and misconduct in their gatherings. That parallel helps explain why Lot's detestation reaches beyond a private preference. He is confronting a public culture in which sexual wrongdoing, aggression, and communal celebration of immorality reinforce one another.

The wider setting does not justify treating every individual as identical. It does show why a prophet must address the community's norms rather than only isolated acts. When a practice is protected by threats and imposed as a social ideal, silence would leave the vulnerable without a truthful defense.

Some people assume that compassion requires avoiding clear words. Lot shows otherwise. He speaks plainly because ambiguity would strengthen the community's self-deception. Compassion tells the truth about danger so that people can leave it; it does not rename the danger as harmless.

At the same time, clarity should not be used to enjoy another person's shame. Lot's words are directed toward correction and separation from wrongdoing. The believer must guard his own heart while speaking, because pride can imitate moral courage and turn hatred of sin into hatred of people.

The people threaten to expel him as if his objection were the offense. Lot refuses that inversion. He will not accept the idea that the person who warns is more blameworthy than the practice being warned against. His reply restores the proper order: wrongdoing is wrong even when a powerful group protects it.

This restoration is especially important where public language has been captured by the dominant culture. Words such as tolerance, freedom, or normality can be used to silence any appeal to revelation. Lot's response shows that a servant may respect people while rejecting the moral vocabulary used to defend disobedience.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:169

> My Lord! Save me and my family from ˹the consequences of˺ what they do.”

**Saving Means Rescue From the Consequences**

The translation makes clear that Lot seeks rescue from what the people do. Their practice is not simply a private disagreement that leaves everyone untouched. It has consequences for the community, for outsiders, and for anyone who refuses to participate. Lot asks God to separate him and his household from the harm generated by that order.

This separation is not contempt for the human beings around him. It is protection from complicity and punishment. A believer may love people while refusing to remain inside a system that demands participation in wrongdoing. Lot's prayer gives language to that difficult need for moral distance.

Quran 29:30 records Lot asking God for help against the people of corruption. The parallel confirms that his response to the community is supplication, not personal revenge. He names the corruption and seeks divine aid because only God can deliver him from a people determined to persist.

The prayer also keeps the messenger from imagining that moral clarity makes him self-sufficient. Lot knows the truth, but he still needs God's protection. Correct belief does not remove vulnerability; it teaches the servant where to take vulnerability.

Lot's concern for his family is natural and honorable, but it is not a request that God overlook wrongdoing for the sake of blood ties. The Qur'anic stories repeatedly separate biological connection from faithfulness. A family is protected by obedience, not by lineage alone.

This principle prevents the prayer from becoming a defense of nepotism. Lot asks for those who belong with him in the path of truth, while divine judgment remains free to distinguish among them. Love does not ask God to call corruption good simply because it occurs within one's household.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:170

> So We saved him and all of his family

**Deliverance Creates Separation From Corruption**

Lot's family is saved by being removed from the environment that has become hostile to God's guidance. Separation here is not an expression of superiority. It is protection from participation in a public evil and from the judgment that will overtake the people who persist in it.

A believer may need distance from a corrupt institution, gathering, or social pressure to preserve obedience. Such distance should be taken with wisdom and without contempt. Lot does not leave because his family is inherently better; he leaves because the community has chosen a path that cannot be shared by those who fear God.

God saves a family together, showing that faith can shape a household's common direction. Shared worship and mutual protection make endurance easier. A family can encourage truth when the surrounding society mocks it and can preserve the vulnerable when public norms become dangerous.

At the same time, the household is not a substitute for individual faith. Each member must respond to God. The rescue of the family is a mercy to people who stand with Lot, not a declaration that every relative is safe regardless of conduct.

**A Parallel Confirms the Pattern**

Quran 7:83 likewise states that God saved Lot and his family except his wife, who was among the doomed. The parallel confirms that the present verse emphasizes a real act of deliverance while the next verse will qualify the household's membership in that deliverance.

The repetition across the accounts also shows that rescue is part of the prophetic pattern. God does not only punish the rejecting community. He preserves those who respond to the warning, even when their numbers are small and their social position is weak.

Lot's family may be surrounded by a powerful majority, but the majority cannot determine whether God will save them. A household can appear isolated and still be held within divine care. The measure is not social strength; it is fidelity to the Lord.

This gives courage to believers who feel outnumbered. They should not mistake loneliness for abandonment or popularity for safety. God's deliverance may be hidden until the moment He chooses, but His knowledge of the faithful is not weakened by the crowd's hostility.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:171

> except an old woman, who was one of the doomed

**Family Relation Does Not Guarantee Salvation**

The previous verse said that Lot and his family were saved; this verse identifies an exception. An old woman from his household is counted among those doomed. The distinction is essential. Divine rescue is not distributed by bloodline alone. A person may live in the home of a prophet and still choose the side of the community that rejects God.

The verse keeps family affection from becoming a false assurance. Lot loved his household and prayed for its protection, but God's judgment remained based on faithfulness and response. The saved group is defined by allegiance to truth, not by a name shared with the messenger.

The woman's old age is mentioned without turning age into an excuse. Human beings deserve care when they are weak or elderly, but age does not make deliberate opposition to God righteous. Vulnerability calls for compassion in human dealings; it does not erase the moral account before God.

This balance prevents two errors. A society must not neglect the elderly because they are weak, and a family must not assume that an elder's position makes every choice beyond question. God knows the circumstances, intentions, and degree of responsibility of each person more fully than any human observer.

**A Household Can Contain Different Allegiances**

Lot's family is not morally uniform simply because it shares a home. One household can contain people who encourage faith and another who identifies with the surrounding culture. The verse teaches the reader to distinguish domestic proximity from spiritual solidarity.

That distinction can be painful. A believer may pray for relatives who do not share his commitment, warn them with wisdom, and still be unable to bring them to faith. Love remains real, but it must not become an argument against God's justice. The Lord does not wrong a person by judging the choice that person knowingly makes.

Quran 7:83 repeats that God saved Lot and his family except his wife, who was among the doomed. Quran 11:81 likewise says that the wife would suffer the fate of the others. These parallels confirm the moral point without requiring later stories to supply details absent from this verse.

The repetition also shows that the exception is not an incidental family dispute. It belongs to the revealed account of how divine rescue distinguishes faith from outward association. The household is mentioned together, but judgment remains attentive to each member's allegiance.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:172

> Then We utterly destroyed the rest

**The Remaining Community Faces Judgment**

After Lot and the faithful household are delivered, the verse turns to the rest of the people. They are utterly destroyed. The contrast is deliberate: the community that threatened the messenger is not allowed to define the outcome, while those who followed God are preserved by His command.

The destruction is not presented as a private victory for Lot or as the result of his personal strength. It belongs to God. Lot warned, prayed, and remained separate from the wrongdoing, but the Lord alone carried out the judgment. The verse keeps divine authority at the center.

The phrase does not simply mean everyone who was still physically present. It refers to those left outside the saving group because of their rejection and corruption. Divine judgment separates people according to allegiance, not according to wealth, age, social rank, or the confidence with which they defended themselves.

That separation may be invisible before the judgment arrives. A crowd can look united while some people have already chosen truth and others have chosen resistance. The rescue and destruction reveal a distinction that God knew all along. Human beings should therefore be humble about appearances and serious about their own response.

**Utter Destruction Is Divine Judgment, Not Human License**

The severity of the verse can tempt a reader to imagine that later people may pronounce the same verdict on their enemies. That would exceed human authority. The Qur'an discloses this judgment as God's act after the community's persistent rejection and corruption. Human beings may resist evil and establish justice, but they do not have the complete knowledge required for God's final sentence.

This distinction is a safeguard against cruelty. The believer learns fear of God from the destruction, not pleasure in another people's ruin. The event should make him cautious with power and eager to prevent wrongdoing before judgment reaches its end.

Quran 29:32 records that the angels knew who was in Lot's city and would save him and his family while the wife was among the doomed. That parallel shows that the division is deliberate and known to God. It is not a confused disaster that happens to catch some people and miss others.

The verse also shows that rescue is not based on numerical strength. A household can be small, while the rest of a city is many and socially dominant. God does not measure truth by the size of the crowd. He saves whom His wisdom identifies as faithful.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:173

> pouring upon them a rain ˹of brimstone˺. How evil was the rain of those who had been warned

**Mercy and Punishment Use the Same Creation**

Rain is a reminder that creation is not autonomous. Water can nourish crops, refresh the land, or, by God's command, become part of a destructive event. People cannot infer divine approval from the ordinary continuation of nature. They must read blessings and warnings through revelation and moral accountability.

This also cautions against treating material conditions as a simple measure of righteousness. A fertile season may be mercy, a test, or a delay before judgment. A disaster may be a consequence, a trial, or something whose full meaning is known only to God. The revealed account of Lot's people is certain, but human beings should not apply its verdict carelessly to every event.

The community had treated its desires as normal and Lot's warning as the offense. The punishment reverses that confidence. What the people had called secure is exposed as fragile, and what they had dismissed as a prophet's objection is confirmed by the event.

The rain therefore falls not only on bodies and buildings but on a false moral order. It ends a public system that had made wrongdoing a condition of belonging and had threatened to expel the truthful. God's judgment restores the distinction between warning and corruption that the people had tried to erase.

Quran 25:40 recalls a city passed by and showered with a dreadful rain, asking whether its ruins were not seen, while noting that the people did not expect resurrection. That parallel connects physical remains with a failure of moral imagination. The ruins were visible, but the lesson could still be ignored.

The present verse calls the rain evil because it was the consequence of a warned community's refusal. Later observers should not turn the ruins into a tourist curiosity detached from accountability. The remnant is a sign only when it leads the heart to fear God and reject the assumptions that produced the judgment.

Without the prophetic warning, the rain might be described only as a catastrophe. With the warning, it becomes a moral event disclosed by God. The community had knowledge, opportunity, and a messenger. Its destruction cannot be explained as though no choice preceded it.

This does not mean that every victim of a natural disaster is personally guilty of the same offense. The verse speaks about this revealed people and this revealed judgment. Its lesson for later readers is humility before God's knowledge and urgency in responding to guidance, not a license to accuse victims of other disasters.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:174

> Surely in this is a sign. Yet most of them would not believe

**The Sign Joins Rescue to Judgment**

A reader who focuses only on destruction misses half the sign. Lot and his family are delivered, showing that God's power is not only the power to end. It is also the power to protect a faithful household when a larger society has become hostile.

The two sides correct each other. Judgment warns the corrupt not to presume, while rescue comforts the obedient not to despair. A complete reading of the event produces fear and hope together. It does not invite a fascination with catastrophe detached from the mercy that preceded it.

Quran 54:34 describes a stone-bearing wind and the deliverance of Lot's family before dawn. The parallel confirms that the event is remembered as both punishment and grace. God does not leave the reader with destruction alone; He points to the rescued people as evidence that obedience has a future.

Quran 25:40 also connects the ruined city with denial of resurrection. The ruins are therefore not only an archaeological trace. They challenge the assumption that death ends accountability. A sign is understood when it expands the horizon beyond the visible remains.

Most people may refuse to believe because a society teaches them how to interpret every sign. If leaders call warning hatred and corruption freedom, individuals may repeat those categories until they seem obvious. Social agreement can make denial feel like common sense.

The verse breaks that comfort. Truth is not created by repetition, and disbelief is not made safe by being widespread. A person must examine the message before the crowd's interpretation becomes his own. Faith requires the courage to stand against a majority when the majority has chosen to resist God.

**The Refusal Is Moral, Not Merely Intellectual**

The phrase “would not believe” indicates resistance, not only lack of information. A person can understand that an account is serious and still reject the surrender it demands. The sign threatens self-rule, so the heart searches for distance from it.

This does not excuse honest questions. Faith can coexist with inquiry and humility. The problem is the settled refusal to let evidence and revelation change conduct. Most of the people remain outside belief because they choose the security of denial over the risk of obedience.

The event is preserved so that later people can respond before facing a similar judgment. A sign is merciful while it gives time for change. Once the consequence has arrived, it confirms the truth but no longer offers the same open future to the destroyed community.

The reader should therefore respond early. Do not wait until a personal collapse proves that pride was dangerous. Let the warning become faith while life, choice, and repentance remain available. Lot's story is a sign because it gives the living an opportunity the dead no longer have.

Those who believe after hearing the account must not boast over those who rejected it. Their belief is itself a mercy from God. The proper response is gratitude, prayer for steadfastness, and concern that others be guided without coercion.

The sign teaches a community to hold truth firmly and speak it wisely. It must not soften the warning to gain popularity, and it must not turn the warning into a weapon of personal hatred. Most may refuse, but the believer remains a servant under the same Lord who judges and saves.

This humility is especially important when discussing a punished people. The sign should make the believer more careful about his own hidden loyalties, not more eager to announce that he would certainly have chosen better. God sees the heart that receives a warning and the heart that merely repeats the right verdict about someone else.

A community that believes the sign will build habits of repentance. It will create room for correction, defend those harmed by public corruption, and refuse to treat comfort as proof of safety. In that way, the event becomes guidance rather than a story admired from a distance.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:175

> And your Lord is certainly the Almighty, Most Merciful

**The Two Names Close Lot's Account**

After the rescue of Lot's household and the destruction of the rest, the verse names the Lord as the Almighty and Most Merciful. The pairing interprets the entire account. God's power explains why the corrupt community could not protect itself, while His mercy explains why Lot was warned, supported, and delivered.

The refrain prevents the story from becoming either a tale of arbitrary force or a promise that mercy means no judgment. God is mighty enough to punish persistent corruption and merciful enough to provide a path of repentance before judgment arrives. Both names are needed to read the event correctly.

Lot's people threatened to expel him, as if their local authority could determine his safety and future. The Almighty shows that the power of a community is temporary. It can threaten a messenger, but it cannot prevent God from saving him or ending the order that opposes His command.

This title also corrects Lot's own vulnerability. The messenger has no need to pretend that he can manage the outcome by himself. He speaks faithfully and turns to the Lord whose power exceeds the crowd's. Courage grows from dependence on the Almighty, not from an illusion of personal invincibility.

**A Parallel Shows Rescue as Grace**

Quran 54:34 describes the punishment of Lot's people and the delivery of his family before dawn as an act of grace. That parallel makes the two names visible in the narrative. Judgment displays divine power, while deliverance displays mercy granted to those who remained with Lot.

The word grace also prevents the saved household from boasting. They were not preserved because they controlled the city or defeated the crowd. God made a way out. The faithful respond with gratitude, not with the belief that their own strength produced the rescue.

The same pair of names closed the stories of Noah, ʿĀd, and Thamûd. Lot's account belongs to a single pattern of prophetic history. Different communities receive different warnings, but the Lord remains consistent: He sends guidance, allows a response, protects those who obey, and judges persistent rejection.

This continuity also teaches that no age can claim exemption. Lot's people had its own customs and desires, just as earlier nations had theirs. The forms of resistance change, but the Almighty remains above every society and the Most Merciful remains near to those who return.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:176

> The residents of the Forest rejected the messengers

**A New Community Is Named by Its Landscape**

The story now turns to the residents of the Forest, or thicket. Unlike the earlier accounts named for Noah, ʿĀd, Thamûd, and Lot, this people is introduced through the place it inhabits. The setting is part of the community's identity, but the opening verdict remains the same: its response to the messengers is rejection.

The landscape may suggest dense trees, a wooded tract, or a place marked by tangled growth. The precise geography is less important to the moral opening than the relationship between a people and its environment. A community can be shaped by its surroundings and still remain accountable for how it responds to God's guidance.

The source tradition preserves a question about whether the residents of the Forest were the same people as Midian or a distinct community to whom Shuʿayb was sent. The Qur'an identifies Shuʿayb as a messenger to Midian in 7:85 and as their brother in 29:36, while other passages name the Forest people separately. The text here does not require a forced resolution.

The cautious conclusion is that the Qur'an places Shuʿayb's warning in a remembered setting associated with a forest or thicket, while leaving the exact relation between that group and Midian open in this verse. The uncertainty does not weaken the message. It directs attention to what the verse makes certain: a community received warning and rejected it.

**Rejection Is the First Fact of the Story**

The verse does not begin by listing the Forest people's trade, homes, or social habits. It begins with what they did with the messengers. That order teaches the reader to judge material and cultural detail through the community's moral response. A people may have a distinctive identity and still be defined by denial if it rejects the Lord's warning.

This opening also creates continuity with the earlier stories. The messenger changes, the environment changes, and the particular temptation will be developed later. The underlying test remains stable. Will the people allow revelation to correct their practices, or will they make their inherited order the final authority?

Shuʿayb's mission is not a random speech addressed to an unknown crowd. Quran 7:85 presents him addressing Midian as their brother and calling them to worship Allah, deal justly, and avoid corruption. That parallel shows the prophetic method: guidance meets a real community in its own setting and addresses the wrongs that shape its common life.

Even if the Forest people and Midian are distinguished in some readings, the parallel reveals the character of Shuʿayb's mission. He is not seeking abstract debate. He calls a community to recognize God and reform the way it uses the gifts placed in its land.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:177

> when Shu’aib said to them, “Will you not fear ˹Allah˺

**Shuʿayb Begins With Reverence**

Shuʿayb addresses the residents of the Forest with the same essential question heard by the earlier communities: will they fear Allah? The question places worship and accountability before the particular social problems that will be discussed. A society cannot repair its dealings while treating God as irrelevant to those dealings.

Taqwa is an awareness that guards the heart from crossing limits. It joins fear of judgment to hope in God's mercy and makes a person careful with wealth, promises, measurement, and power. Shuʿayb's question is therefore the root of the practical reform associated with his mission.

The question is not addressed to an abstract idea of corruption. Shuʿayb speaks to people with a shared place, economy, and identity. Their customs may feel natural because they have been repeated by families and protected by leaders. The question interrupts that familiarity and asks whether the community's way of life can stand before God.

Quran 7:85 presents Shuʿayb speaking to Midian as their brother and calling them to worship Allah, use just measure and weight, and avoid corruption. That parallel shows how reverence becomes social conduct. Fear of God cannot remain in the prayer space while transactions and public decisions ignore His limits.

**Fear of Allah Is Not Fear of People**

A dishonest community may fear losing a customer more than displeasing God. A ruler may fear public opinion more than injustice. Shuʿayb's question reverses that hierarchy. People should care about one another's rights, but their deepest fear must be directed to the Lord who sees every exchange and every hidden motive.

This frees a person from the pressure to imitate wrongdoing. If everyone around him cheats, the fear of being called naive can be strong. Taqwa gives him a greater reason to remain honest. He can accept a worldly loss rather than purchase temporary approval through disobedience.

The people live among trees or a wooded setting, yet a blessed landscape cannot make them automatically grateful. The same environment can sustain honest trade or conceal predatory behavior. Shuʿayb's question shows that moral character is not produced by geography alone.

A community may be proud of its land and resources while treating those gifts as private entitlement. Fear of Allah teaches stewardship. The land, its routes, and its provisions belong ultimately to the Lord, so the people must use them in ways that preserve justice rather than enlarge exploitation.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:178

> I am truly a trustworthy messenger to you

**The Messenger Is Not the Owner of the Message**

The title messenger limits as much as it authorizes. Shuʿayb brings guidance; he does not invent a religion and then demand that people submit to his personality. His trustworthiness is shown by his willingness to remain a servant of the Lord rather than a rival source of worship.

This protects the community from personality cults. People should not obey a messenger because he is charming, powerful, or connected to a respected family. They obey because he faithfully conveys God's command. His personal character supports the message, but the message remains higher than the man who carries it.

Quran 7:85 presents Shuʿayb as sent to Midian and records his call to worship Allah, deal justly, and avoid corruption. That parallel gives concrete shape to the trust he claims. He is not asking the people to abandon ordinary life; he is asking them to conduct ordinary life under divine limits.

Quran 29:36 likewise describes Shuʿayb calling Midian to worship Allah, hope for the Last Day, and avoid corruption in the land. The repeated connection between faith and social repair confirms that his trustworthiness includes the courage to address public wrongdoing rather than retreat into private spirituality.

The next verse will state that Shuʿayb asks no reward, but even here trustworthiness implies independence from purchase. A messenger who can be paid to approve dishonest trade is not a trustworthy guide. Shuʿayb's authority cannot be bought by the people whose interests his message unsettles.

Freedom from payment does not mean the message has no value. It means the people cannot convert guidance into a service sold to the highest bidder. The poor and the rich stand before the same Lord, and neither may purchase a different moral standard.

**Trustworthy Speech Can Threaten Comfortable Arrangements**

The people may have built their prosperity around habits that no longer withstand moral examination. Shuʿayb's trustworthiness requires him to name the problem even if doing so costs him popularity, safety, or social standing. Silence would protect the arrangement but betray the trust placed in him.

This does not make confrontation the messenger's goal. He warns because he wants the people to repent and preserve themselves. A truthful counselor can oppose an institution's practice while still hoping the institution will reform. His firmness is a form of mercy because it prevents polite language from hiding real harm.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:179

> So fear Allah, and obey me

**Obedience Is More Than Formal Compliance**

A person may follow a rule outwardly while searching for a loophole that preserves the same injustice. Shuʿayb's command requires more than technical compliance. It asks the people to obey the moral purpose of God's guidance: honesty, fairness, and protection from exploitation.

This is why obedience begins with fear of Allah. Without reverence, a person may treat rules as obstacles to outsmart. With taqwa, he asks what justice requires even where a narrow reading might allow him to take advantage. The spirit and form of obedience belong together.

Shuʿayb does not call the people to obey him so that he can manage their markets or seize their wealth. He calls them to Allah, whose authority covers both spiritual worship and public exchange. His role is to convey a standard, not to become a new merchant prince.

This distinction matters whenever religious leadership and economic power meet. A true messenger makes people more accountable to God, not more dependent on his personal business. Obedience to Shuʿayb should produce honest dealings among the people rather than a new hierarchy centered on Shuʿayb himself.

**A Parallel Links Worship With Social Repair**

Quran 29:36 records Shuʿayb calling Midian to worship Allah, hope for the Last Day, and avoid corruption in the land. The parallel shows that reverence and obedience are not separate from public reform. Belief in the Last Day gives present conduct lasting significance.

The community cannot claim to fear God while treating the marketplace as a zone without accountability. Nor can it claim to repair society while ignoring worship. Shuʿayb's command joins both: recognize the Lord, then obey the guidance that protects people from one another's greed.

In an unequal exchange, the stronger party can often make the weaker accept terms he would not choose freely. Fear of Allah limits that advantage. It tells the one with wealth, information, or authority that capacity to pressure another person is not permission to do so.

This protection is a practical expression of mercy. A just transaction is not one in which both sides merely sign a document; it is one in which neither party is deliberately deceived or coerced. Shuʿayb's call gives economic life a moral center that human power alone cannot supply.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:180

> I do not ask you for any reward for this ˹message˺. My reward is only from the Lord of all worlds

**Shuʿayb Removes the Suspicion of Profit**

Shuʿayb has challenged the economic conduct of the residents of the Forest. They could claim that he is seeking money or influence through the warning. He answers that he asks no reward from them. His message is not a business arrangement in which they pay for moral advice or purchase a favorable judgment.

The declaration is especially fitting for a messenger addressing a people whose life is connected to exchange. Shuʿayb does not enter their world as another trader seeking a better bargain. He stands outside the transaction and calls them to a Lord whose command cannot be bought or adjusted according to wealth.

Shuʿayb locates his reward with the Lord of all worlds. His hope therefore does not depend on the residents accepting him, praising him, or financing his work. God knows the labor of warning, the pain of rejection, and the sincerity hidden beneath every word.

This independence allows the messenger to speak against practices that benefit powerful people. If his reward came from them, he might soften the call whenever the market, chiefs, or wealthy families objected. By expecting reward from God, he can remain truthful without turning his opposition into personal resentment.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:181

> Give full measure, and cause no loss ˹to others˺

**Worship Is Tested by What the Hand Delivers**

The Qur'an returns to this same pattern in 83:1–3, where those who demand full measure for themselves but reduce what they give others are condemned. The evil is not ignorance of the proper quantity. They know what they want when they are receiving, then choose a different standard when they are giving.

That double standard exposes the heart of the matter. A person may manipulate a mark, a contract, or a calculation, but he cannot hide the moral direction of the choice from Allah. Full measure trains the soul to give others the standard it wants for itself. It turns belief into a habit of exact fairness, one ordinary exchange at a time.

That habit also sets a limit on clever accounting. A contract may use technical language, but a person cannot rely on a customer's inability to read every term and then call the resulting loss voluntary. Consent is meaningful when the terms are clear and the promised good is actually delivered. Shuʿayb's command therefore protects both the visible quantity and the honesty surrounding it.

The same duty reaches beyond a stall. An employer must not make a worker perform hours that are quietly omitted from the record. A lender must not hide a charge that changes the real burden of repayment. A public office must not promise a service and deliver a fraction while keeping the full payment. These examples differ in form, but each asks one party to accept less than the agreed right.

Giving full measure does not demand that every exchange produce identical results. It demands that the result be reached by truthful terms and a fair process. The seller may earn, the buyer may accept risk, and both may benefit from the trade. What cannot be defended is a gain that depends on the other person's ignorance. The scale of the missing amount does not decide whether the act is wrong; the deliberate betrayal of trust does.

This is why honest measurement matters even when the buyer never discovers the shortage. Justice is not created by the victim's ability to complain. The owner of a shop may know that a customer will not return, and an employer may know that a worker cannot challenge a record. Full measure remains due because Allah sees the party who has been made vulnerable. The command gives moral protection where human bargaining power cannot.

A habit of completion can also restore a person's understanding of blessing. Profit gained by reduction may look efficient, but it carries the cost of distrust and the burden of an account before God. A smaller lawful gain can be better than a larger gain built from many hidden losses. Shuʿayb's instruction is therefore not an obstacle placed against prosperity; it is a guard that keeps prosperity from becoming theft.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:182

> Weigh with an even balance

**An Even Balance Refuses a Tilted Standard**

The command moves from the quantity given to the instrument and process by which quantity is judged. An even balance does not favor the person who owns it, the customer who is present, or the trader who knows how to adjust it. Its purpose is to make the same standard govern both sides of the exchange.

The image is simple, but its reach is wide. A person can give a full measure and still manipulate the weighing process before or after it. Shuʿayb therefore addresses the fairness of the method, not only the appearance of the final result. Justice requires a reliable standard from the beginning, so that neither party is forced to trust a private calculation that cannot be examined.

Scales, containers, ledgers, and modern measuring devices are instruments. They do not become dishonest on their own. The moral choice belongs to the person who selects a defective tool, places it on an uneven surface, alters a reading, or presents an unclear unit to someone who assumes it is standard.

This distinction protects a useful principle: technology does not remove accountability. A sophisticated system can conceal a small deduction more effectively than an old scale, but concealment does not make the deduction lawful. The more difficult the method is for an ordinary customer to inspect, the greater the duty of the person who controls it to be transparent.

**Fairness Must Survive Both Directions of Exchange**

An even balance applies when one buys and when one sells. People often demand precision when their own money is leaving and accept approximation when another person's money is entering. The verse rejects that split. The standard must remain stable when the role changes.

The same lesson appears in 17:35, which joins full measurement with an even balance and describes that practice as fairest and best in the end. The phrase about the end gives the command a horizon beyond immediate profit. A dishonest adjustment may produce cash today, while a fair standard produces trust, a clean account, and safety before Allah.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:183

> and do not defraud people of their property. Nor go about spreading corruption in the land

**Other People's Property Is Not Yours to Reduce**

The command names the victim as well as the act. People have property, and the person who deals with it must not quietly turn part of it into his own gain. Defrauding is more than a mistake in arithmetic. It is a knowing reduction of another person's right through deceit, pressure, or misuse of trust.

Quran 2:188 applies the same principle to wealth taken through false means and even mentions using authorities to consume part of another person's property. The reference shows why Shuʿayb's warning cannot be reduced to marketplace weights. A false claim, a corrupt ruling, a hidden fee, and a short measure can share one moral structure: taking what belongs to someone else while pretending that the taking is legitimate.

**Corruption Travels Beyond One Transaction**

The verse then turns from a single act of fraud to a course of conduct in the land. To go about spreading corruption suggests movement, persistence, and public reach. Classical explanations connect the Forest people's wrongdoing with predatory acts such as banditry, raiding, attacks on travelers, and damage to crops. These are not only private sins. They make ordinary life unsafe for everyone who depends on roads, fields, and markets.

Corruption grows when a gain made by one person becomes a fear carried by many. A trader who cheats one buyer harms that buyer; a group that intimidates travelers changes how an entire region lives. The verse joins property and land because private greed can become public disorder.

An isolated dishonest act is serious, but corruption becomes more destructive when leaders protect it, institutions normalize it, or the strong make resistance costly. The victim then loses more than property. He loses the expectation that law, custom, or community will protect him.

This is why 7:85 pairs the prohibition of defrauding property with the prohibition of spreading corruption after the land has been set right. The command does not leave justice to private goodwill. It requires a public order in which trade, authority, and security do not cooperate in the removal of people's rights.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:184

> And fear the One Who created you and ˹all˺ earlier peoples.”

**Fear of the Creator Grounds Every Other Duty**

Shuʿayb now gives the command behind the commands about measure, weight, and property: fear the One who created you. Taqwa is not panic that makes a person flee from God. It is alertness before the Creator that prevents desire, habit, and power from becoming false gods. A person who remembers where his life came from cannot treat another created person as material for his profit.

The warning returns the community from social rules to worship. Fair dealing is not a clever arrangement invented by traders. It is obedience to the One who made both parties and knows the right of each. The people may hide a reduction from a customer, but they cannot hide it from the One who gave them hands, goods, judgment, and time.

The verse does not invoke a local deity tied to one forest or one clan. The Creator made the present people and those who came before them. This wider claim removes the excuse that the community's customs are beyond correction. A practice inherited from ancestors remains answerable to the One who created ancestors and descendants alike.

Quran 7:85 places Shuʿayb's call to worship Allah beside his demand for just measure and weight. The order is significant: recognizing the Creator gives the community a reason to respect created persons. No customer is outside God's care, and no prosperous trader is outside God's judgment.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:185

> They replied, “You are simply bewitched

**The Reply Attacks the Messenger Instead of the Claim**

Shuʿayb has spoken about God, property, and public corruption. The people's first recorded reply does not examine any of those points. It assigns a label to the speaker: bewitched. The accusation tries to make his words seem unworthy of serious consideration before the community has to answer them.

That movement is familiar in disputes about moral reform. When a message threatens a profitable habit, it can be easier to question the speaker's mind than to inspect the habit. The label may produce laughter, fear, or social distance. None of those responses establishes that Shuʿayb's account of justice is false.

**Bewitchment Is Used as a Social Shortcut**

To call someone bewitched can mean that his speech has been affected by an outside force and therefore should not be trusted. It is a shortcut around evidence. The people do not show a defect in his reasoning or answer the demand for honest measure. They try to settle the matter by changing the community's picture of him.

Quran 26:153 records the same accusation against Shuʿayb in this very narrative pattern, and 17:47 preserves a similar charge against the Prophet Muhammad when people heard the Quran. The repetition reveals a recurring defense of denial: make revelation look like confusion, and the hearer will feel permitted to ignore its demands.

Even if a person were ill, distressed, or unusual, that would not make a false measure just. The truth of the command must be tested by its evidence and content, not by an insult directed at the messenger. A community that cares about justice should be especially careful not to use mental or spiritual language as a weapon against a difficult critic.

The accusation also reveals the people's priorities. They are more concerned to discredit Shuʿayb than to deny that they owe others their property. When a society spends its energy policing the reformer's reputation while refusing to inspect its own conduct, the response becomes evidence of moral resistance.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:186

> Also, you are only a human being like us, and we think you are indeed a liar

**Humanity Is Treated as a Reason for Rejection**

The people now present two claims about Shuʿayb. He is human like them, and they regard him as a liar. The first claim is true in one sense: a messenger eats, speaks, and lives among human beings. The error is turning that shared humanity into proof that God could not choose him to carry guidance.

Quran 17:94 records the same objection in direct form: some people found it strange that a human messenger should be sent. The objection misunderstands the purpose of a messenger. If guidance came through a being who never shared human weakness, people could dismiss obedience as impossible to imitate. A human messenger gives the command a visible form within ordinary life.

Because Shuʿayb belongs to their world, the people know his voice, habits, and social position. Familiarity should allow them to test his character, but they use it to reduce him. They see the person they have known and refuse to see the responsibility God may have placed upon him.

The same community can say that someone is too ordinary to be a messenger and too familiar to be trusted. Both judgments avoid the central question: does he speak truthfully for God? Shared humanity is not evidence of lying. It is the condition in which a prophetic example can be understood and followed.

**Calling Him a Liar Is a Verdict Without Examination**

The phrase about lying gives no reason for the verdict. Shuʿayb has not been shown to seek payment, and his warning asks the people to return what belongs to others rather than to enrich himself. His claim must still be assessed, but a bare accusation does not perform that assessment.

A lie about God is a grave charge, so it should not be used as a casual name for an unwelcome speaker. The people have the means to examine his conduct, his message, and its continuity with the call of earlier prophets.
