The home is also where a warning is tested by consistency. A person who calls outsiders to justice while exploiting relatives turns the message into a public costume. A person who teaches the family to pray, repair harm, and protect one another gives the warning a credible shape. Quran 20:132 links family instruction with persistence in prayer, showing that household guidance is patient work rather than a single dramatic speech.

Starting with relatives does not mean ending with them. The nearest circle is the first place where truth becomes embodied, then the warning can reach neighbors, institutions, and the wider society. A family that receives the message can become a source of mercy; a family that rejects it remains answerable despite its closeness to the messenger.

The verse therefore places love under the care of truth. Relatives deserve kindness, protection, and honest counsel, but no human bond can take Allah's place. The messenger warns them because he loves their good and because every person, including the person nearest to him, must meet the Lord with his own response.

The family setting also makes warning a practice of patience. A relative may reject the first explanation, become defensive, or return to the same habit after promising to change. Wisdom does not abandon the truth, but it considers when to repeat, when to listen, and when to set a boundary. The aim is not to win a debate inside the home. It is to make the path of obedience clear and to protect people from the consequences of continued denial.

The verse does not authorize a messenger or parent to use family affection as a weapon. Warning should not say that love is withdrawn unless the person agrees with every personal opinion. It should identify Allah's command, distinguish it from household preference, and leave the hearer responsible before the Lord. That discipline keeps the closest circle from becoming either a sanctuary for wrong or a court of human domination.

Starting with relatives finally reminds public teachers that their credibility is tested in ordinary relationships. A person who is gentle only before an audience has not lowered his wing. A person who warns relatives with truth and patience shows that the message has entered life. The family may accept or reject, but the messenger has fulfilled the duty of bringing the reminder near.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:215

> and be gracious to the believers who follow you

**Mercy Does Not Remove Moral Direction**

Gentleness is not the abandonment of command. The Prophet warns against worshiping other gods, but he also treats believers with graciousness. These duties belong together. A person can be clear about the boundary of faith while speaking to those inside it with patience and respect.

Quran 3:159 describes the Prophet's leniency as a mercy from Allah and links it with pardon, prayer for the believers, consultation, and trust in Allah. That passage confirms that softness is not a weakness added to leadership after the real work is done. It is one of the means by which a community remains attached to guidance rather than fleeing from harshness.

**A Believing Community Needs Tender Strength**

People who follow guidance may still be learning, grieving, or struggling against old habits. Gracious leadership creates room for growth without calling sin acceptable. It corrects clearly, forgives where forgiveness is due, and helps the believer return after failure. Hardness may win a moment of silence, but it can drive the weak away from the very guidance they need.

The verse therefore joins the Prophet's public warning to his private manner. He does not soften the truth for deniers, and he does not harden his heart against believers. The same Lord who is Almighty and Merciful teaches His messenger to carry authority with humility. A community becomes strong when truth is firm, its leader is gentle, and its members are treated as servants of Allah rather than possessions of a human guide.

Gentleness begins with seeing the believer as a person who is still being formed. Someone may have accepted the truth but carry old fears, habits, and wounds. A harsh response to every weakness can make the believer hide his struggle rather than seek help. Lowering the wing creates a safe path to correction. It does not erase the wrong; it makes honest return more likely.

The image also speaks to the relation between leader and community. A leader who stands permanently above people may demand obedience but cannot share their difficulties. The Prophet's humility shows that authority is a trust. He guides toward Allah and remains a worshipper himself. His followers receive direction without being invited to treat him as a source of blessing independent of Allah.

Quran 3:159 connects leniency with pardon, seeking forgiveness for the community, consultation, and trust after decision. That combination shows that gentleness is not indecision. The leader can consult, decide, and act firmly while keeping the hearts of believers close. A hard heart may call itself strong, but the Quran describes hardness as a cause of people scattering.

Graciousness also protects the community from two kinds of faction. The strong should not use religious knowledge to humiliate the weak, and the weak should not demand that leaders approve every desire in the name of kindness. Truth sets the boundary, while mercy determines how the person is approached within that boundary. Both are needed for a faithful community to remain united without becoming morally vague.

The lowered wing finally turns leadership into service. The Prophet is not collecting followers to enlarge a personal kingdom. He is caring for servants who belong to Allah. Anyone who teaches, leads, or corrects should ask whether his manner makes people more attached to Allah or more dependent on his own approval. Prophetic gentleness points beyond itself to the Merciful Lord.

A gracious community also learns how to receive correction. Believers should not demand softness so that no command can challenge them, and leaders should not use truth as an excuse to speak without care. The lowered wing creates a relation in which the believer can admit weakness and the teacher can correct it without turning the correction into a permanent humiliation.

This manner matters when a community grows. New members need welcome, experienced members need humility, and those who have failed need a road back. If the strong guard their status more carefully than they guard the faith of the weak, the community becomes a hierarchy of pride. If everyone is treated as a servant of Allah who can learn and repent, authority becomes a trust rather than a possession.

The command therefore links the public mission to the emotional texture of the believing body. Warning protects truth, while graciousness protects people who are trying to live by it. Both are acts of mercy when they are directed toward Allah. A community that carries the message with firmness and tenderness can correct corruption without reproducing the arrogance it opposes.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:216

> But if they disobey you, say, “I am certainly free of what you do.”

**Disavowal Concerns the Deed, Not a License for Cruelty**

To be free of what they do means to reject the wrongdoing and decline responsibility for it. It does not require the Prophet to act with personal hatred or to deny the humanity of those who disobey. Quran 10:41 gives a parallel distinction between each side's deeds and each side's responsibility.

This distinction matters in moral correction. A person can say that fraud, idolatry, or oppression is unacceptable without claiming that he knows the full hidden account of every wrongdoer. He can protect victims, oppose the act, and still hope that the one who committed it will repent. Separation from wrongdoing is not the same as delight in another person's ruin.

**Freedom From Wrong Protects the Community**

A community needs people who can refuse participation in its corruption. If every relationship requires silent approval, wrongdoing becomes stronger through family loyalty and social fear. The Prophet's declaration teaches that belonging has a limit: no one may demand that a believer endorse disobedience to remain connected.

The words are therefore firm but not despairing. They leave open the possibility that the disobedient will change, while making clear that the messenger will not carry the old conduct as his own. Truthful separation can be an act of mercy to the wrongdoer and protection for the faithful. It tells everyone where responsibility lies before Allah.

To be free of another person's deeds does not mean becoming indifferent to the person. A believer can refuse to join a corrupt practice, warn against it, and still hope that the wrongdoer will repent. The separation is from the act and its moral approval. It is not a command to abandon justice, family duty, or the possibility of reconciliation when the person leaves the wrong.

Quran 10:41 gives a parallel form of this boundary by distinguishing the Prophet's deeds from the deeds of those who reject him. The principle protects both sides. The messenger cannot be blamed for a refusal he opposed, and the opponent cannot borrow the messenger's obedience as a substitute for his own. Every person meets Allah with a record that belongs to that person.

This is especially important for followers who place too much weight on a leader's personality. No teacher can obey on behalf of the student, and no student can transfer his disobedience to the teacher who warned him. Religious guidance creates responsibility rather than removing it. The messenger's disavowal makes that responsibility visible and prevents the community from hiding behind the claim that someone else should have saved it.

The statement also teaches how to leave a harmful group without inventing superiority. A person can say that he is free of a practice because Allah forbids it, while admitting that he too depends on Allah's mercy. He does not need to call himself pure or to predict the final state of everyone who remains. He simply refuses participation and keeps the moral line clear.

When this boundary is maintained, warning remains honest. The Prophet can be gracious to believers and firm toward disobedience without confusing the two. He can invite the wrongdoer back while refusing to carry the wrong as his own. Allah knows the difference between a servant who warned and a person who chose to disobey, and His justice assigns each responsibility correctly.

This boundary is also a protection against inherited guilt. A child is not automatically responsible for a parent's disbelief, and a follower is not automatically innocent because a leader approved the act. People influence one another, but influence does not erase choice. The messenger's disavowal places the deed back with the person who insists on doing it.

At the same time, a person who has influence cannot hide behind this verse and claim that his conduct affects no one. The Prophet is told to warn because guidance and example matter. The disobedient answer for their own deeds, while the person who encouraged or enabled them answers for his own influence. Freedom from another's act is not freedom from one's duty to avoid leading others into it.

The verse leaves a narrow but hopeful path. Reject the wrongdoing now, repair the harm where possible, and do not demand that the messenger or community carry your refusal. If you return to Allah, the earlier separation from the act need not become a declaration that you are beyond mercy. It is a line drawn around the wrong so that the person can step back from it.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:217

> Put your trust in the Almighty, Most Merciful

**The Most Merciful Keeps Trust From Becoming Hardness**

The second name protects trust from turning into severity. The Prophet does not rely on Allah's power so that he can enjoy the downfall of those who oppose him. He relies on the Almighty and Most Merciful, hoping for guidance and leaving room for repentance while warning remains possible.

Quran 3:159 joins gentleness with trust in Allah after a decision has been made. Quran 14:11–12 presents messengers and believers placing trust in Allah while facing opposition. These parallels show that tawakkul contains courage and patience together. The servant is brave before people because Allah is powerful, and gentle toward people because Allah is merciful. He does not need to turn an opponent into a permanent enemy to remain firm, because the outcome belongs to the Lord.

**Tawakkul Holds Means and Outcome Together**

Trust does not mean abandoning planning, counsel, or protection. It means using lawful means without worshiping them. The Prophet warns relatives, teaches believers, and separates himself from disobedience; then he entrusts the result to Allah. A person who refuses every means and calls passivity trust has misunderstood the command.

The verse gives the caller a stable inner posture. Success is not measured only by immediate applause, and rejection is not proof that Allah has lost control. The messenger's duty is faithfulness, while guidance and final judgment belong to the Almighty, Most Merciful. That reliance allows him to keep speaking truth without being ruled by the audience's response.

Tawakkul is often misunderstood as waiting for results without taking steps. The Quranic pattern is different. The messenger warns relatives, shows gentleness, separates from disobedience, and then trusts Allah. The servant locks the door, seeks counsel, speaks honestly, and uses lawful protection, while knowing that none of these means can guarantee an outcome apart from Allah's will.

Reliance is therefore a form of worship expressed through patience when the result is delayed.

The pairing of Almighty and Most Merciful also corrects the emotional extremes of public work. A caller who remembers only power may become harsh and eager to defeat opponents. A caller who remembers only mercy may soften a command until no one knows what is being asked. Trust in both names produces firmness without cruelty and compassion without surrender.

Tawakkul protects the Prophet from taking rejection personally. He cares about the people, but their refusal does not prove that his Lord has abandoned him. He does not measure the worth of revelation by the number of people who accept it in one moment. Allah sees the faithful effort and governs the hearts; the servant continues because the command is true.

For the believer, trust becomes most visible when means appear weak. A person may have little influence, face a powerful wrongdoer, or speak into a community that prefers comfort. He still acts according to truth and leaves the final opening to Allah. The Almighty can change circumstances, and the Most Merciful can preserve a small act of obedience until it bears fruit in ways the servant cannot see.

Trust also teaches the servant how to receive success. If people accept the warning, he thanks Allah rather than treating their acceptance as proof of his personal greatness. If they reject it, he examines his method without surrendering the truth. In both cases, the heart remains attached to Allah, who gives guidance and knows what result is best.

The pairing of the names is especially valuable when a caller faces relatives who oppose him. Power can feel threatening inside a family because rejection touches affection and history. Mercy keeps the messenger from answering pain with revenge. He trusts the Almighty enough to speak clearly and the Most Merciful enough to leave a door open for those who may still return.

A believer's tawakkul is therefore visible in steadiness. He takes means without panic, accepts delay without declaring defeat, and refuses to make public approval his god. The Lord sees the effort, controls the opening of hearts, and judges the motives. Reliance on Him allows a servant to continue doing what is right when the outcome remains outside his hands. He can be patient without becoming passive and decisive without imagining that he controls the hearts of others.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:218

> Who sees you when you rise ˹for prayer at night˺

**Night Worship Nourishes Public Warning**

One interpretation connects the rising with night vigil prayer. Quran 73:20 confirms that the Prophet and some believers stood in prayer at night, while Allah knew their limits and made the practice manageable. The connection places private worship behind public warning. The person who calls people to Allah must return to Allah when no audience is present.

Night prayer is not valuable because darkness itself gives a person spiritual status. Its value lies in the unobserved turning of the servant toward his Lord. It trains sincerity, patience, and dependence. A messenger who rises before people rise receives strength from the One who sees him before the community hears him.

**Prayer Anchors the Messenger's Authority**

The Prophet does not rise as an independent reformer who draws authority from his own resolve. He rises before the Almighty, Most Merciful, and receives the mission as a servant. Prayer keeps warning connected to worship and protects leadership from becoming a search for control over people.

The verse therefore gives the believer a way to understand religious work. Public teaching, family counsel, and opposition to corruption need a private return to Allah. The Lord sees that return and knows whether the outward call grows from worship or from the desire to be admired. The rising is hidden from people but fully visible to the One who gives the servant strength.

If the verse refers to night prayer, the hidden hour gives special meaning to the Prophet's public endurance. The community sees a warning delivered in daylight, but Allah sees the servant who stood when sleep and comfort invited him to remain in bed. Quran 73:20 confirms the reality of the Prophet's night standing and Allah's knowledge of the differing capacities of the worshippers. Private worship is not a separate life from public mission; it is its source of steadiness.

If the verse refers to rising as a prayer leader, the same reassurance remains. The Prophet stands before a congregation, but he does not stand outside Allah's sight. Every movement is seen, every intention known, and every act of guidance remains worship only when it is directed to Allah. Leadership in prayer is therefore service, not a claim to spiritual ownership of the people behind the leader.

The two readings can also illuminate one another. A person who rises privately is prepared to lead publicly without vanity, and a leader who stands before a congregation must preserve the private sincerity that public prayer can conceal. Whether the worship is hidden or shared, Allah sees the servant before, during, and after the visible act.

This divine sight gives the Prophet courage to warn his relatives and mercy toward believers. He does not need the family to approve him or the congregation to praise him. The Lord sees the rising that makes the warning possible. A believer who works for Allah can therefore accept that some of the most important parts of service will remain unknown to people.

The verse also corrects the idea that religious effort is measured by public reach alone. A person may teach many and still need a private return to Allah; another may pray unseen and influence no public institution while being precious before his Lord. The All-Seeing Lord values the act according to sincerity and obedience, not according to the noise surrounding it.

Private rising also gives a warning about the source of religious confidence. Public knowledge can be gathered quickly, and a leader can learn to speak in ways that move a crowd. Night prayer tests what remains when the crowd is absent. The servant discovers whether he wants Allah or only the effect that religious language produces on people.

This does not make hidden worship a reason to despise public service. The Prophet's mission requires both. He rises before Allah and then warns people; he leads or teaches in public while preserving the private bond that keeps the work sincere. The two dimensions correct one another. Private prayer without concern for people can become isolation, while public concern without private prayer can become performance.

The verse gives reassurance to anyone whose sincere effort is unseen. A parent who rises to pray before caring for the household, a teacher who asks Allah for help before speaking, and a believer who quietly resists a wrong are all within divine sight. Their work does not need a human audience to become real. Allah sees the rising and knows what it produces in the servant.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:219

> as well as your movements ˹in prayer˺ along with ˹fellow˺ worshippers

**Allah Sees Prayer in Its Shared Movement**

The verse extends divine sight from the Prophet's rising to his movements among worshippers. Prayer is not an abstract intention detached from the body. Standing, bowing, prostrating, and moving with the congregation all occur under Allah's knowledge. The physical pattern becomes an act of shared obedience rather than a private performance.

The wording has received several interpretations. Some connect the movement with the Prophet's night worship and his movement among those who prayed with him. Others understand it as the movements of a prayer leader alongside the worshippers who follow him. Another reading gives movement a broader sense, including the Prophet's attention moving across the congregation. The common point is Allah's complete sight of worship and worshippers.

**Congregation Makes Humility Visible**

When worshippers move together, rank and wealth are temporarily placed under one order. The leader is followed, but he remains a worshipper; the follower obeys the prayer's order, but he stands before Allah directly. The verse's shared setting shows that leadership in worship is not ownership of the people who follow.

This matters for the Prophet's public role. He warns relatives and separates from disobedience, yet he also stands among believers in worship. His authority does not remove him from servanthood. The congregation sees a messenger leading, while Allah sees every heart and every movement within that leadership.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:220

> He ˹alone˺ is indeed the All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**Hearing and Knowing Complete the Reassurance**

The passage closes with two divine attributes. Allah is All-Hearing and All-Knowing. He hears the Prophet's recitation, prayer, warning, and private appeal, and He knows the intention, effort, and response connected with each act. Nothing is lost because it was spoken softly, performed at night, or ignored by the audience.

The word alone in the translation reinforces exclusivity. The Prophet trusts the One who hears and knows, not a rival power and not the approval of people. This completes the command to trust the Almighty, Most Merciful. His power is joined to perfect perception, so His care is never blind and His judgment is never based on incomplete information.

**Allah Hears More Than Public Speech**

The All-Hearing hears the warning made in public and the prayer made in solitude. He hears the believer who can explain his faith and the person whose only response is a quiet cry. This attribute gives hope to people whose truth is not recognized by society. A person's voice may be dismissed by the crowd without being lost before Allah.

It also gives weight to every word. A person cannot hide a false promise by speaking it where no witness stands. A leader cannot erase a cruel command because the victims were too afraid to repeat it. Allah hears the words that create justice and the words that create harm, including the tone and purpose humans may fail to perceive.

**Allah Knows the Movement Behind the Act**

Knowledge reaches the inward reason for worship. Two people may make the same movement in prayer while one seeks Allah and the other seeks reputation. Human observers can see the form, but Allah knows the heart. The pairing of hearing and knowing prevents the believer from reducing accountability to visible performance.

Quran 6:19 presents Allah as the witness between the Prophet and his people and the Quran as a warning that reaches whoever it reaches. The same principle appears here at the close of prayer and mission. Allah knows who conveyed faithfully, who listened honestly, and who turned the reminder into an excuse for denial.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:221

> Shall I inform you of whom the devils ˹actually˺ descend upon

**The Guarded Revelation Has a Different Descent**

Quran 72:8–9 describes the jinn finding heaven guarded and their former attempts at eavesdropping blocked. That parallel supports the contrast in this verse. The devils do not have free access to the heavenly source of revelation. What reaches the Prophet is brought by permission through a trustworthy channel, not gathered by hostile listeners.

The question therefore prepares the reader to distinguish two kinds of descent. Revelation descends with truth and protection; satanic influence descends upon people already prepared to lie and sin. The difference is not only where something comes from in the unseen. It is also what the influence produces in the human being who receives it.

The wording shall I inform you is also a teaching method. It invites the audience to compare the accusation with the evidence rather than simply endure a denial. The people called the Quran demonic, but the Quran asks them to look at the actual receivers of satanic influence. Their moral character is part of the answer. A source that produces deliberate falsehood is not the source of a message that calls people to truth.

Influence can be understood as a relation of desire. The devil does not make a truthful heart into a liar by force. He finds a person who wants permission to lie, then offers a story that makes the lie easier to accept. The person's readiness does not excuse the influence; it explains why the influence gains room. Repentance begins by closing that room through truthfulness and obedience.

The question also warns against judging a message by the unusual nature of its delivery alone. The unseen is not a sign of evil, and ordinary speech is not automatically a sign of truth. A claim must be measured by its source, its chain, its content, and its fruits. The Quran gives these measures openly, while the satanic channel is associated with hidden fragments and habitual falsehood.

A believer should therefore be careful when people use the word demonic as a weapon. The label may be intended to frighten the audience away from a teaching before the teaching is examined. Ask what the teaching commands, what kind of character it forms, and whether it brings people toward Allah or toward pride and harm. The verse does not invite superstition; it invites moral discernment.

The opening question stands as a final answer to the old accusation. The Quran is not authenticated by the fact that it astonishes people, nor refuted by the fact that opponents dislike it. Its source is the Lord, its carrier is trustworthy, and the beings who truly descend upon corrupt liars are excluded from its protected transmission. The reader is left with a practical test: examine the claim, examine its moral fruit, and refuse to let an accusation replace honest attention to revelation. What comes from Allah calls the heart into truth; what comes through satanic influence seeks a heart already willing to betray it. That distinction is both theological and practical, because every listener must choose which influence to welcome, nourish, and obey in every moment daily. Discernment is proved not by suspicion of the unseen but by faithful response to what Allah has made clear.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:222

> They descend upon every sinful liar

**Truthful Speech Is a Protection**

The Quran gives a practical contrast to this type of receiver. A servant who values truth admits ignorance, corrects an error, and refuses to turn revelation into a tool for personal gain. Such a servant may still be tempted, but he does not build a home for falsehood in the heart.

Quran 49:6 commands verification when news comes from an evildoer, because unexamined information can lead to harm and regret. The instruction assumes that character affects how reports should be received. It does not license suspicion of everyone; it teaches careful truth-seeking rather than surrender to the sinful liar's version of events.

**The Description Separates Prophecy From Soothsaying**

A prophet conveys revelation under Allah's protection. A sinful liar uses unseen claims, invented reports, or persuasive speech to gain influence. The two should not be confused because both may speak about matters people cannot immediately see. The Quran's trustworthy chain and moral call distinguish it from the claims of those upon whom devils descend.

The verse therefore answers the accusation at its root. The devil's receiver is a habitual sinner who fabricates, while the revelation calls people to worship, justice, repentance, and accountable speech. The source of a message is tested by its truth and direction, not by the excitement created when it enters a crowd.

The description every sinful liar also exposes a false kind of charisma. A speaker may be quick, confident, and entertaining while carrying no concern for truth. The crowd may mistake certainty of tone for certainty of knowledge. The Quran refuses that measure. Character and conduct matter before the drama of delivery.

The sinful liar can also make other people sinful by normalizing falsehood. If a leader invents a claim to protect wealth, followers may repeat it to protect belonging. If a performer turns slander into entertainment, listeners may learn to treat another person's honor as a cheap source of amusement. The influence spreads through willing repetition, but the first responsibility remains with the person who chooses to fabricate.

A truthful person may have a strong imagination or an unusual way of speaking. Those qualities do not make him a receiver of devils. The dividing line is not plainness versus creativity. It is whether speech is submitted to Allah and whether the speaker is willing to correct himself when the truth opposes his desire. Sincerity can coexist with eloquence; it cannot coexist comfortably with deliberate lying.

The verse also teaches how to respond when a report comes from a morally compromised person. Do not spread it because it contains one detail that fits what you already believe. Verify it, seek another account, and consider who would be harmed if it were false. Quran 49:6 makes this discipline explicit. Careful verification is an act of justice, not a failure of trust.

The distinction between prophet and soothsayer becomes clear at this point. The prophet is bound by revelation and accountable to the Lord who sends him. The soothsayer or sinful liar uses claims of hidden access to gain authority while remaining willing to deceive. The Quran's protection from satanic descent preserves the first role from being confused with the second.

The verse therefore calls for a culture in which truth is more valuable than a thrilling report. A community that rewards the liar will receive more lies. A community that honors repentance, evidence, and honest correction makes less room for the influence described here. The moral answer to satanic deception begins with refusing to become its audience.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:223

> who gives an ˹attentive˺ ear ˹to half-truths˺, mostly passing on sheer lies

**Truth Needs Humility and Restraint**

The right response to uncertain information is not to decorate it with confidence. Say what is known, mark what is uncertain, and stop the report when passing it on would harm people. The person who does this resists the path described in the verse, even when rumor would bring attention or advantage.

The contrast is decisive. The satanic channel thrives on fragments and lies; the believer seeks complete truth and accountability before Allah. Listening becomes worship when it leads to verified obedience. It becomes corruption when it turns half-heard claims into a market of false certainty.

The verse's structure shows how a false report travels. There is a receiver who gives an ear, a source that supplies fragments, and a public that receives the final story. Responsibility exists at every stage. The source may deceive, the listener may fail to question, and the repeater may add confidence that was never present in the original. Falsehood grows as it moves because each person wants the report to do something for him.

This is why the Quran does not praise curiosity without discipline. A person may want secret information because it makes him feel important or gives him influence over people who cannot check his claim. The desire to know the unseen can become a desire to control the seen. Revelation closes that route by teaching that the protected message is received through a trustworthy chain, not through private eavesdropping.

The word mostly also guards against a crude conclusion that no information ever reaches such a listener. A fragment of truth can be used to make a lie believable. The believer should not be impressed by the occasional accuracy of a source whose general method is deception. A correct detail does not validate the authority of the carrier or justify the harm caused by the false majority around it.

The same discipline applies to public speech. Do not repeat a claim about a person because it sounds spiritually serious, and do not attribute secret knowledge to a speaker merely because he predicts something that later occurs. Verify what can be verified, admit what cannot, and protect people's honor from reports that have not earned confidence. Quran 49:6 gives this principle a direct social form.

The contrast with revelation is therefore complete. The Quran is not a rumor assembled from overheard fragments. It is a protected message brought by the trustworthy spirit and conveyed to a prophet for warning. The believer honors that message by listening carefully, learning its context, and refusing to make uncertain speech compete with clear guidance.

An attentive ear should finally be an ethical ear. It should hear the suffering a rumor may cause, the duty a command creates, and the correction Allah is sending. When listening produces humility and action, it becomes a path to truth. When it produces fascination, slander, and false certainty, it repeats the pattern exposed by the verse.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:224

> As for poets, they are followed ˹merely˺ by deviants

**The Verse Leaves Room for Beneficial Speech**

Later passages distinguish between harmful poets and those who believe, do good, remember Allah, and defend the wronged. That exception shows how to read the present verse. The condemned pattern is not the mere use of rhythm, metaphor, or emotional force. It is the alliance of speech with deviation, falsehood, and conduct that contradicts truth.

Words are a trust whatever form they take. A poem can glorify injustice, or it can expose it. A speech can manipulate a crowd, or it can call people back to Allah. The test is not whether language is beautiful, but whether beauty serves truth and whether the person who follows the words is led toward righteousness or further wandering.

The verse should therefore be read against the specific accusation facing the Prophet. His opponents did not use poet as a neutral description. They used it to deny that the Quran was revelation and to place the Prophet among people whose words were attributed to inspiration, imagination, or unseen companions. Quran 52:29–30 records the same kind of pressure, contrasting the prophetic reminder with the labels of fortune-teller, possessed person, and poet.

The Quran answers by showing that its speech has a fixed purpose. It warns, clarifies, corrects, and calls people to Allah. A poet in the condemned pattern can change allegiance when the audience changes, because the performance is built around effect. Revelation does not change its truth to preserve a patron. It can praise a believer, condemn a tyrant, comfort the weak, and warn the messenger while remaining one message from one Lord.

The presence of imagery does not erase this difference. Human poetry can use metaphor for truth or falsehood, just as ordinary prose can deceive. The category is about source, purpose, and moral role rather than about whether a sentence is vivid. Quran 36:69 calls the Book a reminder and clear Quran, making its function explicit: it brings people to awareness and warning rather than to admiration of a human poet.

The verse also challenges the audience that follows speech uncritically. Deviants are not only people with a certain political label. They are those who let the speaker's excitement replace the question of where the words lead. A crowd can follow a talented voice into pride, lust, tribal hatred, or false accusation. The beauty of the performance then hides the ugliness of the destination.

A believer may appreciate eloquence while remaining morally alert. He can enjoy a poem, story, or speech without treating it as guidance in every matter. He asks whether the words are true, whether they honor people, and whether the speaker practices the values being praised. The Quran's exception in the final verse confirms that form alone is not the issue; the issue is whether language is governed by faith and good action.

Poetry is therefore not saved by a label or condemned by one. It is judged by the Lord who hears every word. The verse protects revelation from a false classification and teaches the community to treat verbal beauty as a trust rather than an authority independent of Allah.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:225

> Do you not see how they rant in every field

**A Wide Range of Subjects Can Hide a Narrow Motive**

The speaker may appear versatile because he can move through politics, love, tribal memory, religion, and satire. Yet the motive can remain the same: gain attention, protect status, or defeat an enemy. Variety of subject does not prove depth of thought. It can be a way of keeping the audience from testing the speaker's consistency.

Quran 18:103–104 describes people whose efforts go astray while they think they are doing good. That parallel warns that confidence and productivity do not guarantee a sound direction. A person can produce much speech, win admiration, and still be moving away from truth if his work is detached from obedience and honest self-examination.

**A Moral Center Gives Speech Direction**

Speech guided by Allah can address many subjects without losing its center. It can praise what is true, criticize what is wrong, and use imagination without making falsehood beautiful. Its variety serves a stable purpose: remembrance, justice, and preparation for the return to Allah.

The verse therefore asks the listener to look past verbal motion. Do not be impressed simply because a speaker can occupy every field. Ask where the speech leads, whom it serves, and whether the speaker will obey the truth when obedience costs him. Movement becomes guidance only when it has a right direction.

The image of valleys also suggests exaggeration without rootedness. A speaker can describe every emotional landscape but never stand on a principle when standing becomes costly. He praises peace when the crowd wants peace and praises conflict when conflict brings applause. The words move quickly, but the person has not moved toward truth.

This instability harms the audience because it rewards attention rather than integrity. Listeners learn to follow the strongest mood in the room. One day they despise an enemy, the next day they praise him, and the day after they forget the harm caused by both reactions. The speaker's range becomes a substitute for a stable moral account.

A truthful communicator can change his judgment when new evidence appears. That is not the wandering condemned here. Correction is a sign of humility when it follows truth. The problem is changing positions to follow appetite while pretending that each new position was always held. Honest revision has reasons; verbal wandering has only momentum.

The passage helps distinguish sincere energy from sound direction. Effort, productivity, and applause cannot prove that a speaker's field is right. A person may work hard for a false cause and become more attached to it because of the work already spent.

The listener therefore needs a pause between performance and response. Ask what claim is being made, what evidence supports it, and what conduct it would produce if accepted. A field of speech becomes a field of guidance when the words can survive those questions. If they require constant excitement to remain persuasive, they may be carrying the audience away from reflection.

The verse does not demand that every speaker use one tone or one subject. It demands a center that remains true across subjects. A writer can be tender and forceful, descriptive and direct, as long as Allah's truth governs the movement. The valley is not the danger; losing the road is.

A community that values this discipline will enjoy language without becoming captive to it. It will welcome imagination that illuminates justice and reject cleverness that disguises falsehood. Speech can travel widely while the heart remains anchored to Allah, and that anchored movement is different from the wandering exposed here.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:226

> only saying what they never do

**Words Can Become a Substitute for Obedience**

A powerful description of good can make a person feel close to goodness without doing it. The audience applauds the speech, the speaker enjoys the applause, and the moral work is postponed. In that way language becomes a substitute for action rather than a guide toward action.

Quran 61:2–3 directly condemns saying what one does not do, and Quran 2:44 rebukes those who preach righteousness while forgetting themselves. These parallels show that the problem is not limited to poets. Poetry provides a vivid case because its emotional power can make a false life appear noble, but the same contradiction can affect scholars, leaders, parents, and ordinary believers. Sacred vocabulary does not cancel the obligation to embody the values being proclaimed.

**Practice Gives Language Its Moral Weight**

The Quran does not separate speech from action. A warning should lead to repentance, praise should lead to gratitude, and a call to justice should lead to fair dealing. The person who says what he does not do has broken that connection and made language serve vanity.

The verse therefore calls the listener to a disciplined sincerity. Speak less if necessary, but let what is said be supported by effort. When failure occurs, acknowledge it and return to Allah rather than defending the image. Words become guidance when they are joined to deeds, and deeds become clearer when truthful words direct them.

The gap between word and deed is especially dangerous in a religious setting. A speaker may use sacred language to gain trust, then exploit the trust for money, status, or control. The audience may assume that religious vocabulary proves religious character. The verse teaches the opposite: claims must be tested by conduct, and conduct must be measured by Allah's commands.

This does not require a person to wait until he is flawless before he advises anyone. A servant can remind others while admitting that he is also struggling. The difference lies in honesty and direction. He does not present himself as the finished embodiment of a standard while secretly defending the opposite. He welcomes correction and keeps working to make his life agree with his speech.

The cure is self-application: every command spoken to others must first be heard as a command to the speaker.

The audience also bears responsibility. It should not follow a famous speaker into wrong merely because he has said beautiful things. Nor should it reject a true principle simply because the person presenting it has failed. Separate the truth of the command from the credibility of the example, then demand that leaders repair the gap between them.

Action gives speech a history. A promise kept over time, a wrong admitted, and a sacrifice made when no one applauds can give simple words great weight. Conversely, repeated contradiction can make even a true phrase sound empty in the speaker's mouth. Allah knows the difference between a servant who fails and returns and a performer who has no intention of returning.

The verse therefore calls for integrity rather than theatrical perfection. Say what is true, do what is possible, admit what has not been done, and keep moving toward obedience. The distance between words and deeds should become a place of repentance and effort, not a permanent stage on which the speaker deceives others and himself.

## Sūrah al-Shuʿarāʾ 26:227

> Except those who believe, do good, remember Allah often, and ˹poetically˺ avenge ˹the believers˺ after being wrongfully slandered. The wrongdoers will come to know what ˹evil˺ end they will meet

**Remembrance Keeps Creative Speech Accountable**

Remembering Allah often does not mean repeating words mechanically while the heart remains absent. It means keeping Allah's presence, commands, mercy, and judgment active in the choices made by the speaker. Quran 33:41 commands believers to remember Allah often, so remembrance is a broad discipline that shapes speech, action, and attention.

For a poet, remembrance can restrain vanity. Praise cannot become worship of a patron, satire cannot become permission to lie, and emotional force cannot become a license to injure the innocent. The imagination remains active, but it is not sovereign. It serves truth rather than demanding that truth serve the imagination.

**Defending the Wronged Is Not the Same as Revenge**

The translation speaks of poetically avenging believers after they have been wronged. The context is a response to slander and attack, not permission for uncontrolled retaliation. A just defense answers falsehood with truth, protects the honor of the innocent, and stops the aggressor from turning lies into accepted history.

Quran 42:39–40 describes believers who enforce justice when wronged and also praises pardon and reconciliation when they are appropriate. That balance clarifies this exception. A response to slander may be necessary, but it must remain proportionate and free of fresh injustice. The believer does not become righteous by defeating one lie with ten lies.

**The Final Warning Returns to the Wrongdoers**

The verse ends by warning those who do wrong that they will know their final return. This gives the exception its moral boundary. The believing speaker may answer slander, but the wrongdoer is not defeated merely by a clever poem or public embarrassment. Every person returns to Allah, where the whole account of speech, harm, defense, and intention is known.

The ending also keeps the righteous from pride. Defending believers does not make a person immune from judgment. He must continue to believe, do good, and remember Allah while responding to wrong. The best speech is therefore joined to a good life and a sober awareness of the homecoming awaiting both the slanderer and the defender.

The exception is carefully built. Belief without action can become a claim, action without remembrance can become self-righteous activism, and remembrance without concern for the wronged can become private comfort. The verse joins them so that speech serves a complete moral life. The believing poet is not saved by a noble theme alone; his character and conduct must support the defense he makes.

To avenge the believers after being wronged also requires truth about what happened. A person cannot call every criticism oppression and then use religious language to silence correction. Quran 42:39–40 balances resistance to aggression with pardon and reconciliation where they are better. A just response protects people without making the defender a new wrongdoer.

The phrase remember Allah often keeps the defense from becoming tribal revenge. The believer answers a slander because honor and truth matter before Allah, not because he wants to enlarge his faction. He can expose a lie without lying about the opponent, and he can resist an attack without turning every disagreement into a license for hatred.

The warning to wrongdoers at the end widens the verse beyond poetry. Anyone who harms, slanders, manipulates, or abuses words has a return to face. The final homecoming is not controlled by the applause of a crowd. Allah knows who was wronged, who defended truth, who exceeded the limit, and who hid behind a beautiful phrase.

This makes the exception a standard for all creative and public speech. Believe, do good, remember Allah, and use talent to repair rather than corrupt. The form may be a poem, a speech, a post, or a story; the moral test remains the same. Language becomes honorable when it stands with truth and when the speaker is willing to live under the truth he proclaims.

The final verse therefore does not merely rescue a small group from a general condemnation. It gives the community a way to transform verbal power into service. The righteous speaker may be imaginative, forceful, and emotionally vivid, but he remains accountable to Allah. His words defend the wronged without worshiping anger, and his life gives the words the weight that empty performance cannot supply.
